8.50012pt\textsc{rIfSkip}\par\frac{c\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{skip}:\mathcal{Q}\land\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{Q}\hskip 8.50012pt\mbox{for all $e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c$ in $gcs$}}{\mathsf{do}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od}\mathbin{\>\mid\>}\mathsf{skip}:\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{Q}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}\mbox{{enab}}(gcs)\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{rDoSkip}\par\frac{c\mathbin{\mid}d:\mathcal{R}_{i}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{S}\mbox{ for all $i\in X$}\hskip 8.50012pt\mbox{$X$ finite}}{c\mathbin{\mid}d:(\lor\,i\>:\>i\in X\>:\>\mathcal{R}_{i})\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{S}\\
}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{rDisjN}\par\par

Figure 3.
Some derived rules of RHL+.

Figure 2
gives the proof rules of the
\forall\forall
logic RHL+.
The
\forall\exists
logic appears in
section 9
;
until then we focus on
\forall\forall
.
We are not aware of a prior RHL that has been formulated for GCL,
but the rules are straightforward adaptations of rules found in prior work.
In particular,
rIf
generalizes the standard 4-way if-rule:
instead of
2\times 2
cases there are
i\times j
where
i
and
j
are the number of branches left and right.

Rules such as the same-branch-if rule popular in RHLs
[
Ben04
,
Yan07
]

are easily derived, see
rAlgnIf
and
rAlgnDo
in

Figure 3
. It is also easy to derive
rules relating different control structures, in particular the “one sided” rules like
rSeqSkip
and
rSeqIf

in
Figure 3
. These are useful for reasoning by sequential alignment which is embodied in rules
rLRseq
and
rRLseq
.

Rule
rDoSkip
is derived as follows.
Observe that
\mathsf{skip}\mathrel{\simeq}\mathsf{do}\ {\mathit{false}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathsf{skip}}\ \mathsf{od}
,
so we get the conclusion of
rDoSkip
by
rRewrite
from

\mathsf{do}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od}\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{do}\ {\mathit{false}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathsf{skip}}\ \mathsf{od}:\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{Q}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}\mbox{{enab}}(gcs)\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
.
This we prove using
rDo
with
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}:=\mathit{true},\mathit{false}
.
The left-only premises have the form

c\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{skip}:\mathcal{Q}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\land\mathit{true}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{Q}
.
They follow from the premises of
rDoSkip
using
rConseq
to
add the conjunct
\mathit{true}
.
6
6

6

By shallow embedding,
rConseq
is
never needed to manipulate equivalent relations, but we mention it for clarity.

The right-only and joint premises are proved by
rFalse
and
rConseq
.
The side condition of this instantiation of
rDo
simplifies to

\mathcal{Q}\Rightarrow(\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}\mbox{{enab}}(gcs)\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}=\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}\mathit{false}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$})\lor\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}\mbox{{enab}}(gcs)\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}

and the consequent simplifies to true.
7
7

7

The side condition in
rDo
uses notation

\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}\mbox{{enab}}(gcs)\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}=\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}\mbox{{enab}}(gcs^{\prime})\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
for a store relation that some readers may prefer to write as
\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}\mbox{{enab}}(gcs)\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\Leftrightarrow\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}\mbox{{enab}}(gcs^{\prime})\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
. Similarly for the side conditions of
rAlignIf
and
rAlignDo
.

Proofs of the other rules in
Figure 3
are similar.

Unlike some RHLs in the literature, RHL+ does not make use of unary correctness judgments.
However, unary correctness can be encoded as relational correctness, in the sense that
validity of
c:P\leadsto Q

is equivalent to validity of the judgment

c\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{skip}:\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}P\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}Q\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}

and also

\mathsf{skip}\mathbin{\mid}c:\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}P\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}Q\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
.
We return to this in
subsection 7.2
.

{exa}

Consider these programs adapted from
[
NN21
]
(and in turn from
[
Fra83
]
).

\begin{array}[]{ll}\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$c4:$}&y:=x;z:=24;w:=0;\\
&\mathsf{do}\ {y\neq 4\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathsf{if}\ {w\mathbin{\mathsf{mod}}2=0\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}z:=z*y;y:=y-1\talloblong w\mathbin{\mathsf{mod}}2\neq 0\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathsf{skip}}\ \mathsf{fi};w:=w+1}\ \mathsf{od}\\[1.1625pt]
\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$c5:$}&y:=x;z:=16;w:=0;\\
&\mathsf{do}\ {y\neq 4\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathsf{if}\ {w\mathbin{\mathsf{mod}}3=0\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}z:=z*2;y:=y-1\talloblong w\mathbin{\mathsf{mod}}3\neq 0\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathsf{skip}}\ \mathsf{fi};w:=w+1}\ \mathsf{od}\end{array}

For input
x
with
x\geq 4
these compute factorial and exponent respectively. Without reasoning about those functions, however, one can show that factorial majorizes exponent for arguments at least 4. Formally:

c4\mathbin{\mid}c5:\mathbb{A}x\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}x\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\geq 4\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}z\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}>\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}z\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
.
The reader may enjoy to use the proof rules to prove the spec,
using rule
rDo
with loop alignment conditions
\mathcal{L}:=\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}w\mathbin{\mathsf{mod}}2\neq 0\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}
and
\mathcal{R}:=\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}w^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathsf{mod}}3\neq 0\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
.
As a loop invariant, try
\mathbb{A}y\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}y\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\geq 4\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}z\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}>\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}z\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}z\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}>0
.

Another approach is to rewrite the loops, unfolding them 2 (resp. 3) times.
This is not possible, however, if we replace literals 2 and 3 by
an input variable
v
with precondition
\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}v\geq 1\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}v\geq 1\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
.
Of course the example is contrived, but such data-dependent alignment patterns arise in settings such as equivalence checking for optimizing compilers.
∎

4.
Alignment automata, adequacy, and verification conditions

This section lays groundwork for alignment completeness, defining alignment automata
for
\forall\forall
properties and the verification conditions for program alignment automata.
The definitions are adapted to
\forall\exists
properties in
subsection 9.1
.

4.1.
Automata, alignment automata and adequacy

We adapt a number of technical definitions from
[
NN21
]
,
where automata are formulated in a way that can represent program semantics using, in essence, a finite control flow graph.

{defi}

An
automaton
is a tuple

(Ctrl,Sto,\mathit{init},\mathit{fin},\mapsto)

where
Sto
is a set (called the data stores),

Ctrl
is a finite set (the control points)
that contains distinct elements
\mathit{init}
and
\mathit{fin}
,
and
{\mapsto}\subseteq(Ctrl\times Sto)\times(Ctrl\times Sto)
is the transition relation.
8
8

8

The symbol
\mapsto
here has nothing to do with
the store update notation, e.g.,
s[x\mbox{$\,\mapsto\,$}v]
, where the square brackets
should prevent any confusion.

We require

(n,s)\mapsto(m,t)
to imply
n\neq\mathit{fin}
and
n\neq m

and call these the
finality
and
non-stuttering
conditions respectively.
Absence of stuttering loses no generality and facilitates definitions involving product automata.
A
state
of the automaton is an element of
Ctrl\times Sto
.

We use the term “alignment product” informally, in reference to various constructions in the literature.
We define a particular construction that we call alignment automaton.

{defi}

Let
A=(Ctrl,Sto,\mathit{init},\mathit{fin},\mapsto)
and

A^{\prime}=(Ctrl^{\prime},Sto^{\prime},\mathit{init}^{\prime},\mathit{fin}^{\prime},\mapsto^{\prime})
be automata.
Let
L
,
R
, and
J
be subsets of
(Ctrl\times Ctrl^{\prime})\times(Sto\times Sto^{\prime})

that are
live for

A,A^{\prime}
, meaning that:

\begin{array}[t]{l}\forall((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in L.\>(n,s)\in\mbox{{dom}}(\mapsto)\\
\forall((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in R.\>(n^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in\mbox{{dom}}(\mapsto^{\prime})\\
\forall((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in J.\>(n,s)\in\mbox{{dom}}(\mapsto)\land(n^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in\mbox{{dom}}(\mapsto^{\prime})\end{array}
.

The
alignment automaton

\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J)
is
the automaton

((Ctrl\times Ctrl^{\prime}),(Sto\times Sto^{\prime}),(\mathit{init},\mathit{init}^{\prime}),(\mathit{fin},\mathit{fin}^{\prime}),\Mapsto)

where
\Mapsto
is defined by:

((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\Mapsto((m,m^{\prime}),(t,t^{\prime}))
iff one of these conditions holds:

LO:

((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in L
and

(n,s)\mapsto(m,t)
and
(n^{\prime},s^{\prime})=(m^{\prime},t^{\prime})

RO:

((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in R
and

(n,s)=(m,t)
and
(n^{\prime},s^{\prime})\mapsto^{\prime}(m^{\prime},t^{\prime})

JO:

((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in J

and
(n,s)\mapsto(m,t)
and
(n^{\prime},s^{\prime})\mapsto^{\prime}(m^{\prime},t^{\prime})

Notice that the states of
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J)
are

((Ctrl\times Ctrl^{\prime})\times(Sto\times Sto^{\prime}))
.
So the
alignment conditions

L
,
R
, and
J
are sets of alignment automaton states.
We write
[n|n^{\prime}]
for the set of states where control is at
(n,n^{\prime})
, i.e.,

\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$[n|n^{\prime}]$}\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\{((i,i^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\mid n=i\land n^{\prime}=i^{\prime}\}
.
For example
[\mathit{fin}|\mathit{fin}^{\prime}]
is the set of terminated states.
Let
\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$[n|*]$}\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\{((i,i^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\mid n=i\}
.

Roughly speaking, taking
L
and
R
to be false and
J
true, we obtain an automaton that runs
A
and
A^{\prime}
in lockstep.
Taking
L,R,J
all true, we obtain a nondeterministic alignment automaton that represents very many alignments.
Taking
L,R,J
all false, we obtain an alignment automaton that represents no alignments whatsoever.
Taking
J
to be false,
L
to be
[*|\mathit{init}^{\prime}]
, and
R
to be
[\mathit{fin}|*]
, we obtain an automaton that runs only
A
, unless it terminates, in which case it proceeds to run
A^{\prime}
---the sequential alignment.
9
9

9

To be precise,
one must ensure that
L,R,J
are live (per
subsection 4.1
).
Later we construct automata from programs, and for such automata every state has a successor except when control is final (
7
).
Hence, for programs, an arbitrary triple
(L,R,J)
can be made live very simply, as

(L\setminus[\mathit{fin}|*],R\setminus[*|\mathit{fin}^{\prime}],J\setminus[\mathit{fin}|\mathit{fin}^{\prime}])

where
\setminus
is set subtraction.

{defi}

Consider an alignment automaton
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J)

and relation
\mathcal{P}\subseteq Sto\times Sto^{\prime}
.
The alignment automaton is
\mathcal{P}
-adequate
provided for
all
(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}
and
t,t^{\prime}
with

(\mathit{init},s)\mapsto^{*}(\mathit{fin},t)
and
(\mathit{init}^{\prime},s^{\prime})\mapsto^{\prime*}(\mathit{fin}^{\prime},t^{\prime})
,
we have

((\mathit{init},\mathit{init}^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\Mapsto^{*}((\mathit{fin},\mathit{fin}^{\prime}),(t,t^{\prime}))
.

An alignment automaton may be adequate for reasons that are specific to the
underlying automata, for example it may not cover all traces but still cover all outcomes.
We focus on alignment automata that are adequate in the sense that they cover
all traces, which can be ensured as follows.
Given a relation
\mathcal{P}\subseteq Sto\times Sto^{\prime}
, an alignment automaton is

manifestly
\mathcal{P}
-adequate
provided
that
L\lor R\lor J\lor[\mathit{fin}|\mathit{fin}^{\prime}]
is
\mathcal{P}
-invariant
.
This means
L\lor R\lor J\lor[\mathit{fin}|\mathit{fin}^{\prime}]
holds at every state reachable from some

((\mathit{init},\mathit{init}^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))
such that
(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}
.

Lemma 1
.

If alignment automaton
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J)
is manifestly
\mathcal{P}
-adequate
then it is
\mathcal{P}
-adequate (in the sense of
subsection 4.1
).

4.2.
Correctness of automata, and the inductive assertion method

Generalizing slightly from
section 3
,
we consider specs
P\leadsto Q
where
P
and
Q
are sets of automaton stores,
not necessarily variable stores.
Satisfaction of a spec by an automaton is written

A\models P\leadsto Q
and defined to mean:
For all
s,t
such that
(\mathit{init},s)\mapsto^{*}(\mathit{fin},t)
,
if
s\in P
then
t\in Q
.

Let
A
and
A^{\prime}
be automata with store sets
Sto
and
Sto^{\prime}
respectively.
Generalizing slightly from
section 3
,
we consider relational specs
\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{S}

where
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{S}
are relations from
Sto
to
Sto^{\prime}
.
Satisfaction of the spec by the pair
A,A^{\prime}
is written

A,A^{\prime}\models\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{S}
and defined
to mean, for any
s,s^{\prime},t,t^{\prime}
:

If
(\mathit{init},s)\mapsto^{*}(\mathit{fin},t)
and
(\mathit{init}^{\prime},s^{\prime})\mapsto^{\prime*}(\mathit{fin}^{\prime},t^{\prime})

and
(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
then
(t,t^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}
.

A store relation
\mathcal{Q}
for
A,A^{\prime}
can be seen as a predicate on the stores
of an alignment automaton
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J)
because the latter are pairs of stores.
Hence, for relational spec
\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{S}
,
the unary spec
\mathcal{Q}\leadsto\mathcal{S}
makes sense
for
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J)
.

Lemma 2

(adequacy semantically sound and complete)
.

Suppose that
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J)
is
\mathcal{Q}
-adequate.
Then
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J)\models\mathcal{Q}\leadsto\mathcal{S}

if and only if

A,A^{\prime}\models\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{S}
.

Given automaton
A
and spec
P\leadsto Q
,
an
annotation
is a function
an
from control points to store predicates
such that
P\Rightarrow an(\mathit{init})
and
an(\mathit{fin})\Rightarrow Q
.
The requirement
\mathit{init}\neq\mathit{fin}
in
subsection 4.1
ensures that annotations exist for any spec.
10
10

10

In Floyd’s formulation, an annotation only needs to be defined on a subset of control points that cut every loop in the control flow graph. Such an annotation can always be extended to one for all control points.

We lift
an
to a function
\hat{an}
that yields states:

\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$\hat{an}(n)$}=\{(m,s)\mid s\in an(n),m\in Ctrl\}
.
Put differently:

(m,s)\in\hat{an}(n)\quad\mbox{iff}\quad s\in an(n)\qquad\mbox{(for all $m,n,s$)}

(4)

For each pair
(n,m)
of control points
there is a
verification condition
(
VC
):

\mbox{{post}}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n,m}}{{\longmapsto}})(\hat{an}(n))\subseteq\hat{an}(m)

(5)

Here
post
gives the direct image (i.e., strongest postcondition) of a relation,
11
11

11

Defined for any set
X
and relation
R

by
t\in\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$\mbox{{post}}(R)(X)$}
iff
\exists s.\>(s,t)\in R\land s\in X
.

and
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n,m}}{{\longmapsto}}
is the
fixed-control transition relation
restricted to starting control point
n
and ending
m
, i.e.,

(i,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n,m}}{{\longmapsto}}(j,t)\;
iff
\;i=n
,
j=m
, and
(n,s)\mapsto(m,t)

(6)

It is well known that (
5
) is equivalent to
\hat{an}(n)\subseteq\mbox{{wp}}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n,m}}{{\longmapsto}})(\hat{an}(m))

using the universal preimage operator
wp
.
12
12

12

Defined for any set
X
and relation
R
by
s\in\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$\mbox{{wp}}(R)(X)$}
iff
\forall t.\>(s,t)\in R\Rightarrow t\in X
.

The VC (
5
) says that for every transition from control point
n
and store
s\in an(n)
,
if the step goes to control point
m
with store
t
, then
t
is in
an(m)
.
Annotation
an
is
valid
if the VC is true for every pair
(n,m)
of control points.
In most automata, including those we derive from programs, some pairs
(n,m)
have no transitions,
in other words
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n,m}}{{\longmapsto}}
is empty.
In that case the VC (
5
) is true regardless of
an(n)
and
an(m)
.

In case the stores of
A
are variable stores,
a set
S
of
A
-states is
finitely supported
provided that
for each control point
n
the set of stores
\{t\mid(n,t)\in S\}
is finitely supported. Similarly for states of an alignment product.
We say
A
is finitely supported if its transition relation acts on finitely many variables.
13
13

13

This can be formalized as follows:
\mbox{{dom}}(\mapsto)
is finitely supported and
for any
x
outside the support of
\mbox{{dom}}(\mapsto)
and any
states
(n,s)
and
(m,t)
, if

(n,s)\mapsto(m,t)
then

(n,s[x\mbox{$\,\mapsto\,$}i])\mapsto(m,t[x\mbox{$\,\mapsto\,$}i])

for all
i\in{\rm Nature}
.

An annotation
an
is finitely supported provided
an(n)
is, for all control points
n
.

Lemma 3

(semantic soundness and completeness of IAM
[
Flo67
]
)
.

There is a valid annotation of
A
for
P\leadsto Q
iff

A\models P\leadsto Q
.
Moreover, in case
A
acts on variable stores and
P,Q
are finitely supported,

A\models P\leadsto Q
implies there is a finitely supported valid annotation.

Corollary 4

(soundness of alignment automata)
.

Suppose that
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J)
is
\mathcal{Q}
-adequate and

an
is an annotation of
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J)
for
\mathcal{Q}\leadsto\mathcal{S}
.
If
an
is valid then
A,A^{\prime}\models\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{S}
.

Together,
4
and
1

provide a method to verify

A,A^{\prime}\models\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{S}
:
Find alignment conditions
L,R,J
and annotation
an
of
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J)

such that the annotation is valid and

\breve{an}(i,j)\Rightarrow L\lor R\lor J\lor[\mathit{fin}|\mathit{fin}^{\prime}]
for every
i,j
.
This uses the abbreviation

\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$\breve{an}(n,n^{\prime})$}\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\hat{an}(n,n^{\prime})\land[n|n^{\prime}]
.
This implication ensures manifest adequacy.

Corollary 5

(semantic completeness of alignment automata)
.

Suppose
A,A^{\prime}\models\mathcal{S}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{T}
.
Then there are
L,R,J
and a valid annotation
an
of
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J)
for
\mathcal{S}\leadsto\mathcal{T}

such that
\breve{an}(i,j)\Rightarrow L\lor R\lor J\lor[\mathit{fin}|\mathit{fin}^{\prime}]
for every
i,j
.
Moreover,
if
A
and
A^{\prime}
act on variable stores, and
A,A^{\prime},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{T}
are finitely supported,
then so are
L
,
R
,
J
, and
an
.

4.3.
Automata from programs and their VCs

\par\frac{}{\langle\mathsf{hav}^{n}\ {x},\>s\rangle\rightarrowtriangle\langle\mathsf{skip}^{-n},\>s[x\mbox{$\,\mapsto\,$}v]\rangle}\par\frac{}{\langle\mathsf{skip}^{n};c,\>s\rangle\rightarrowtriangle\langle c,\>s\rangle}\par\frac{e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c\mbox{ is in }gcs\hskip 8.50012pt\llbracket\,e\,\rrbracket(s)=\mathit{true}}{\langle\mathsf{if}^{n}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{fi},\>s\rangle\rightarrowtriangle\langle c,\>s\rangle}\par\frac{\mbox{{enab}}(gcs)(s)=\mathit{false}}{\langle\mathsf{do}^{n}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od},\>s\rangle\rightarrowtriangle\langle\mathsf{skip}^{-n},\>s\rangle}\par\frac{}{\langle x:=^{n}e,\>s\rangle\rightarrowtriangle\langle\mathsf{skip}^{-n},\>s[x\mbox{$\,\mapsto\,$}\llbracket\,e\,\rrbracket(s)]\rangle}\par\frac{e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c\mbox{ is in }gcs\hskip 8.50012pt\llbracket\,e\,\rrbracket(s)=\mathit{true}}{\langle\mathsf{do}^{n}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od},\>s\rangle\rightarrowtriangle\langle c;\mathsf{do}^{n}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od},\>s\rangle}\par\frac{\langle c,\>s\rangle\rightarrowtriangle\langle d,\>t\rangle}{\langle c;b,\>s\rangle\rightarrowtriangle\langle d;b,\>t\rangle}\par

Figure 4.
Transition semantics (with
n
and
v
ranging over
\mathbb{Z}
).

Labels on commands serve as basis for defining the automaton for a program,
to which end we make the following definitions (adapted from
[
NN21
]
).
Write
\mbox{{ok}}(c)
to say no label in
c
occurs more than once and all labels are positive.
Write
\mbox{{lab}}(c)
for the label of
c

and
\mbox{{labs}}(c)
for the set of labels that occur in
c
.
(The only non-obvious case is
\mbox{{lab}}(c;d)\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\mbox{{lab}}(c)
,
see
Figure 22
.)
For small-step semantics,
we write
\langle c,\>s\rangle\rightarrowtriangle\langle d,\>t\rangle
if command
c
with store
s
transitions to continuation command
d
and store
t
(see
Figure 4
).

Negative labels are used in the small-step semantics
in a way that facilitates defining the automaton of a program.
In a configuration reached from an
ok
command,
the only negative labels are those introduced by the transition for assignment
and the transition for termination of a loop.
For every
c,s
, either
\langle c,\>s\rangle
has a successor via
\rightarrowtriangle

or
c
is
\mathsf{skip}^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. (This relies on
total
-
if
of Def.
3
.)
When a negative label is introduced, the configuration is either terminated
or has the form
\langle\mathsf{skip}^{-n};c,\>s\rangle

in which case the next transition is

\langle\mathsf{skip}^{-n};c,\>s\rangle\rightarrowtriangle\langle c,\>s\rangle
.

Lemma 6
.

For any
c,s,t
, we have

\llbracket\,c\,\rrbracket\,s\,t

iff

\langle c,\>s\rangle\rightarrowtriangle^{*}\langle\mathsf{skip}^{n},\>t\rangle

for some
n
.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\;}c@{\;}l}\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,c;d,f)&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,c,\mbox{{lab}}(d))\mbox{ , if $n\in\mbox{{labs}}(c)$}\\
&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,d,f)\mbox{ , otherwise}\\
\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,\mathsf{if}^{n}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{fi},f)&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&f\\
\mbox{{fsuc}}(m,\mathsf{if}^{n}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{fi},f)&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\mbox{{fsuc}}(m,c,f)\mbox{ , if $e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c\in gcs$ and $m\in\mbox{{labs}}(c)$}\\
\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,\mathsf{do}^{n}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od},f)&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&f\\
\mbox{{fsuc}}(m,\mathsf{do}^{n}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od},f)&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\mbox{{fsuc}}(m,c,n)\mbox{ , if $e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c\in gcs$ and $m\in\mbox{{labs}}(c)$}\\
\lx@intercol\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,\mathsf{skip}^{n},f)\;\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\;\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,x:=^{n}e,f)\;\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\;\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,\mathsf{hav}^{n}\ {x},f)\;\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\;f\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\end{array}

Figure 5.
Following successor
\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,c,f)
,
assuming
\mbox{{ok}}(c)
,
n\in\mbox{{labs}}(c)
, and
f\notin\mbox{{labs}}(c)
.

Write
\mbox{{sub}}(n,c)
for the sub-command of
c
with label
n
, if
n
is in
\mbox{{labs}}(c)
.
Let
c
and
\mathit{fin}
be such that
\mbox{{ok}}(c)
and
\mathit{fin}\notin\mbox{{labs}}(c)
.
We write
\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,c,\mathit{fin})
for the
following successor
of
n
in the control flow graph of
c
, i.e., the control successor of the subprogram at
n
,
in the sense made precise in
Figure 5
.
Note that
\mathit{fin}
serves as a final or exit label.
The key case in the definition is for loops: the following successor is the control point after termination of the loop.
For the running example, we have
\mbox{{fsuc}}(2,c0,6)=6
and
\mbox{{fsuc}}(4,c0,6)=2
.
Define
\mbox{{okf}}(c,f)
(“ok, fresh”) to abbreviate the conjunction
of
\mbox{{ok}}(c)
and
f\notin\mbox{{labs}}(c)
.

If
\mbox{{okf}}(c,f)
then we define the
automaton of
c
for
f
, written
\mbox{{aut}}(c,f)
, with control set

\mbox{{labs}}(c)\cup\{f\}
and transitions in accord
with the small-step semantics.

{defi}

Suppose
\mbox{{okf}}(c,f)
.
The
automaton of
c
for
f
, written
\mbox{{aut}}(c,f)
, is

(\mbox{{labs}}(c)\cup\{f\},(\mbox{{Var}}\to\mathbb{Z}),\mbox{{lab}}(c),f,\mapsto)

where

(n,s)\mapsto(m,t)
iff either

\bullet

\exists d.\>\langle\mbox{{sub}}(n,c),\>s\rangle\rightarrowtriangle\langle d,\>t\rangle\land\mbox{{lab}}(d)>0\land m=\mbox{{lab}}(d)
, or

\bullet

\exists d.\>\langle\mbox{{sub}}(n,c),\>s\rangle\rightarrowtriangle\langle d,\>t\rangle\land\mbox{{lab}}(d)<0\land m=\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,c,f)
, or

\bullet

\mbox{{sub}}(n,c)=\mathsf{skip}^{n}\land m=\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,c,f)\land t=s

The first two cases use the semantics of
Figure 4

for a sub-command on its own.
The second case uses
fsuc
for a sub-command that takes a terminating step
(either assignment, havoc, or loop).
The third case handles skip, which on its own would be stuck
but which should take a step when it occurs as part of a sequence.
14
14

14

For example, with
\rightarrowtriangle
we have

\langle\mathsf{skip}^{n};\mathsf{skip}^{m},\>s\rangle\rightarrowtriangle\langle\mathsf{skip}^{m},\>s\rangle

but
\mathsf{skip}^{n}
by itself has no transitions and the first two cases
do not apply. Owing to the third case we have
(n,s)\mapsto(m,s)\mapsto(f,s)
.
This also shows that the automaton steps are not in exact correspondence with
those via
\rightarrowtriangle
but this does not matter.

{exa}

c3
on page

in
section 2
,
using 12 as final label, is depicted in
Figure 6
.
One can see an alignment automaton for
c3
in
Figure 17
,
ignoring the dashed boxes in the figure.

Automata for program
c3
(in

\mbox{{aut}}(c3,f)
where

.

Lemma 7
.

The only stuck states of
\mbox{{aut}}(c,f)
are terminated ones, i.e., where the control is
f
.

Lemma 8

(automaton consistency)
.

Suppose
\mbox{{okf}}(c,f)
and let
n=\mbox{{lab}}(c)
.
For any
s,t
we have

\llbracket\,c\,\rrbracket\,s\,t
iff
(n,s)\mapsto^{*}(f,t)
in
\mbox{{aut}}(c,f)
.
Hence

\models c:P\leadsto Q
iff
\mbox{{aut}}(c,f)\models P\leadsto Q

for any
P,Q
.
Also
\models c\mathbin{\mid}c^{\prime}:\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{S}
iff
\mbox{{aut}}(c,f),\mbox{{aut}}(c^{\prime},f^{\prime})\models\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{S}

for any
\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{S}
and
\mbox{{okf}}(c^{\prime},f^{\prime})
.
The same holds for the
\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}
judgment.

By inspection of the transition semantics in
Figure 4
,
there are six kinds of transitions
for
\rightarrowtriangle
and also for the automaton relation
\mapsto
derived from it.
(There is a seventh rule for
\rightarrowtriangle
that says a transition can occur for the first command in a sequence, but that is used together with one of the other six,
and it is not relevant to
subsection 4.3
.)
Thus there are six kinds of verification conditions, which can be derived from
the semantic definitions.

if
\mbox{{sub}}(n,c)
is…

and
m
is…

then the VC for
(n,m)
is equivalent to…

\mathsf{skip}^{n}

\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,c,f)

an(n)\Rightarrow an(m)

x:=^{n}e

\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,c,f)

an(n)\Rightarrow{an(m)}^{x}_{e}

\mathsf{hav}^{n}\ {x}

\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,c,f)

an(n)\Rightarrow\dot{\forall}x.\>an(m)

\mathsf{if}^{n}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{fi}

\mbox{{lab}}(d)
where
e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}d
is in
gcs

an(n)\land e\Rightarrow an(m)

\mathsf{do}^{n}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od}

\mbox{{lab}}(d)
where
e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}d
is in
gcs

an(n)\land e\Rightarrow an(m)

\mathsf{do}^{n}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od}

\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,c,f)

an(n)\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\mbox{{enab}}(gcs)\Rightarrow an(m)

In all other cases, there are no transitions from
n
to
m
so the VC is
true
by definition.

VCs for the automaton
aut(c,f)
of

program

and annotation
an
.

Lemma 9

(VCs for programs)
.

Consider
c
and
f
such that
\mbox{{ok}}(c,f)
, and
let
an
be an annotation of
\mbox{{aut}}(c,f)
.
For each pair
n,m
of labels, the VC of equation (
5
)
can be expressed as in
Figure 7
.

Proof 4.1
.

We give two cases. The other cases are similar.

Case

\mathsf{skip}^{n}
with
m=\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,c,f)
.
To show: the VC is equivalent to
an(n)\Rightarrow an(m)
.
The VC of (
5
) is the first line of the following calculation.

\begin{array}[]{lll}&\mbox{{post}}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n,m}}{{\longmapsto}})(\hat{an}(n))\subseteq\hat{an}(m)\\
\Leftrightarrow&{\qquad\mbox{def of $\mbox{{post}}$}}\\
&\forall i,s,j,t.\>(i,s)\in\hat{an}(n)\land(i,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n,m}}{{\longmapsto}}(j,t)\;\Rightarrow\;(j,t)\in\hat{an}(m)\\
\Leftrightarrow&{\qquad\mbox{def $\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n,m}}{{\longmapsto}}$ and one-point rule of predicate calculus}}\\
&\forall s,t.\>(n,s)\in\hat{an}(n)\land(n,s)\mapsto(m,t)\Rightarrow(m,t)\in\hat{an}(m)\\
\Leftrightarrow&{\qquad\mbox{def (\ref{eq:hatALT})}}\\
&\forall s,t.\>s\in an(n)\land(n,s)\mapsto(m,t)\Rightarrow t\in an(m)\\
\Leftrightarrow&{\qquad\mbox{def $\mapsto$ for the case of skip with successor $m$ (\autoref{def:aut})}}\\
&\forall s,t.\>s\in an(n)\land s=t\Rightarrow t\in an(m)\\
\Leftrightarrow&{\qquad\mbox{one-point rule}}\\
&\forall s.\>s\in an(n)\Rightarrow s\in an(m)\end{array}

The last line is equivalent to
an(n)\subseteq an(m)
which we write as
an(n)\Rightarrow an(m)
.

Case

\mathsf{hav}^{n}\ {x}
with
m=\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,c,f)
.
To show: the VC is equivalent to
an(n)\Rightarrow\dot{\forall}x.\>an(m)
.
As in the previous case, the VC is equivalent to the first line of this calculation:

\begin{array}[]{lll}&\forall s,t.\>s\in an(n)\land(n,s)\mapsto(m,t)\Rightarrow t\in an(m)\\
\Leftrightarrow&{\qquad\mbox{using def $\mapsto$ for case $\mathsf{hav}^{n}\ {x}$ with successor $m$}}\\
&\forall s,v.\>s\in an(n)\Rightarrow s[x\mbox{$\,\mapsto\,$}v]\in an(m)\\
\Leftrightarrow&{\qquad\mbox{using def of substitution}}\\
&\forall s,v.\>s\in an(n)\Rightarrow s\in{an(m)}^{x}_{v}\\
\Leftrightarrow&{\qquad\mbox{by predicate calculus}}\\
&\forall s.\>s\in an(n)\Rightarrow\forall v.\>s\in{an(m)}^{x}_{v}\\
\Leftrightarrow&{\qquad\mbox{by def}}\\
&\forall s.\>s\in an(n)\Rightarrow s\in\dot{\forall}x.\>an(m)\end{array}

which we write as
an(n)\Rightarrow\dot{\forall}x.\>an(m)
.

4.4.
Relational VCs

Consider an alignment automaton
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J)
where the underlying automata

and

are obtained from programs by

.
An annotation of

thus maps pairs of control points of
A,A^{\prime}
to

Verification conditions are associated with tuples
((n,n^{\prime}),(m,m^{\prime}))

that represent edges in the control flow graph of
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J)
,
i.e., VCs are given by (
5
) instantiated with
n:=(n,n^{\prime})
and
m:=(m,m^{\prime})
.

For a given pair
((n,n^{\prime}),(m,m^{\prime}))
of alignment automaton control points,
the transitions go only via LO, or only via RO, or only via JO in
subsection 4.1
.
If
n=m
, i.e., control does not change on the left, the transitions must be via RO
because the non-stuttering condition for automata (
subsection 4.1
)
ensures there is no unary transition where control does not change.
Similarly, if
n^{\prime}=m^{\prime}
the transitions are via LO.
If
n\neq m
and
n^{\prime}\neq m^{\prime}
the transitions only go via JO.

gives the six VCs for transitions that go by the LO condition.

There are 36 combinations for JO transitions of an alignment automaton; some of their VCs are in

.
By contrast with
Figure 7

and
9
we do not eliminate lift (hat) notation
in
Figure 8
and
Figure 9
,
because the alignment conditions
L
,
R
,
J
are sets of states, not sets of stores.
We return to this later, using the
pc
variable to encode the control part of the state.
In
Figure 8
and
Figure 9
, the substitution notation is lifted from store relations to state relations and likewise for
\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}
and
\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
.

Lemma 10

(VCs for program alignment automata)
.

Consider programs
c
and
c^{\prime}
, with alignment automaton

\prod(\mbox{{aut}}(c,f),\mbox{{aut}}(c^{\prime},f^{\prime}),L,R,J)
.
Let
an
be an annotation.
For each pair
(n,n^{\prime}),(m,m^{\prime})
of alignment automaton control points, the VC
can be expressed
as in
Figure 9
,
Figure 8
, and in the omitted RO cases that are symmetric to
Figure 8
.

Proof 4.2
.

First, for any
(n,n^{\prime})
and
(m,m^{\prime})
the VC is
equivalent to the first line of this calculation:

\begin{array}[]{lll}&\mbox{{post}}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(n,n^{\prime}),(m,m^{\prime})}}{{\Mapsto}})(\hat{an}(n,n^{\prime}))\subseteq\hat{an}(m,m^{\prime})\\
\Leftrightarrow&{\qquad\mbox{definitions}}\\
&\forall i,i^{\prime},s,s^{\prime},j,j^{\prime},t,t^{\prime}.\>\begin{array}[t]{l}((i,i^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in\hat{an}(n,n^{\prime})\land((i,i^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(n,n^{\prime}),(m,m^{\prime})}}{{\Mapsto}}((j,j^{\prime}),(t,t^{\prime}))\\
\Rightarrow((j,j^{\prime}),(t,t^{\prime}))\in\hat{an}(m,m^{\prime})\end{array}\\
\Leftrightarrow&{\qquad\mbox{using def (\ref{eq:hatALT}) of hat}}\\
&\forall i,i^{\prime},s,s^{\prime},j,j^{\prime},t,t^{\prime}.\>\begin{array}[t]{l}(s,s^{\prime})\in an(n,n^{\prime})\land((i,i^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(n,n^{\prime}),(m,n^{\prime})}}{{\Mapsto}}((j,j^{\prime}),(t,t^{\prime}))\\
\Rightarrow(t,t^{\prime})\in an(m,m^{\prime})\end{array}\\
\Leftrightarrow&{\qquad\mbox{using def (\ref{eq:tranSeg})}}\\
&\forall i,i^{\prime},s,s^{\prime},j,j^{\prime},t,t^{\prime}.\>\begin{array}[t]{l}(s,s^{\prime})\in an(n,n^{\prime})\land(i,i^{\prime})=(n,n^{\prime})\land(j,j^{\prime})=(m,m^{\prime})\\
\land\>((i,i^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\Mapsto((j,j^{\prime}),(t,t^{\prime}))\Rightarrow(t,t^{\prime})\in an(m,m^{\prime})\end{array}\\
\Leftrightarrow&{\qquad\mbox{predicate calculus (the one-point rule)}}\\
&\forall s,s^{\prime},t,t^{\prime}.\>\begin{array}[t]{l}(s,s^{\prime})\in an(n,n^{\prime})\land((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\Mapsto((m,m^{\prime}),(t,t^{\prime}))\\
\Rightarrow(t,t^{\prime})\in an(m,m^{\prime})\end{array}\hfill(7)\end{array}

From here we proceed by distinguishing the three cases discussed preceeding this lemma in Sec.
4.4
, depending on whether
n=m
,
n^{\prime}=m^{\prime}
, or neither equality holds.

In case
n^{\prime}=m^{\prime}
, the LO case applies and unfolding the definition of
\Mapsto
in (
4.2
) we get

\forall s,s^{\prime},t.\>\begin{array}[t]{l}(s,s^{\prime})\in an(n,n^{\prime})\land((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in L\land(n,s)\mapsto(m,t)\\
\Rightarrow(t,s^{\prime})\in an(m,n^{\prime})\end{array}

(8)

In case
n=m
, the RO case applies, which is similar.
In case
n\neq m
and
n^{\prime}\neq m^{\prime}
, the JO case applies and unfolding
\Mapsto
in (
4.2
) we get

\forall s,s^{\prime},t,t^{\prime}.\>\begin{array}[t]{l}(s,s^{\prime})\in an(n,n^{\prime})\land((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in J\land(n,s)\mapsto(m,t)\land(n^{\prime},s^{\prime})\mapsto(m^{\prime},t^{\prime})\\
\Rightarrow(t,t^{\prime})\in an(m,m^{\prime})\end{array}

From here, we must consider for each LO case the six possible commands
\mbox{{sub}}(n,c)
and successors
m
,
for each RO case the possible commands
\mbox{{sub}}(n^{\prime},c^{\prime})
and successors
m^{\prime}
,
and for each JO all 36 combinations.

Here is one case: LO where
\mbox{{sub}}(n,c)
is
x:=^{n}e
,

m=\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,c,f)
, so the VC is for
((n,n^{\prime}),(m,n^{\prime}))
.
By definition of
\mapsto
, (
8
) is equivalent to

\forall s,s^{\prime},t.\>\begin{array}[t]{l}(s,s^{\prime})\in an(n,n^{\prime})\land((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in L\land t=s[x\mbox{$\,\mapsto\,$}\llbracket\,e\,\rrbracket(s)]\Rightarrow(t,s^{\prime})\in an(m,n^{\prime})\end{array}

which equivales (by def of semantic substitution and one-point rule)

\forall s,s^{\prime}.\>\begin{array}[t]{l}(s,s^{\prime})\in an(n,n^{\prime})\land((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in L\Rightarrow(s,s^{\prime})\in{an(m,n^{\prime})}^{x|}_{e|}\end{array}

which, using def (
4
) of lift and def of the control predicate
[n|n^{\prime}]
, is equivalent to

\forall i,i^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}.\>\begin{array}[t]{l}((i,i^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in\hat{an}(n,n^{\prime})\land((i,i^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in[n|n^{\prime}]\land((i,i^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in L\Rightarrow\\
((i,i^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in{\hat{an}(m,n^{\prime})}^{x|}_{e|}\end{array}

Now that the condition is phrased uniformly in terms of state sets,
we can write it as

L\land\hat{an}(n,n^{\prime})\land[n|n^{\prime}]\Rightarrow{\hat{an}(m,n^{\prime})}^{x}_{e}
,
and even more succinctly as

L\land\breve{an}(n,n^{\prime})\Rightarrow{\hat{an}(m,n^{\prime})}^{x|}_{e|}
using the
\breve{an}
abbreviation.
This concludes the proof for the left assignment case in
Figure 8
.
(Note: It is for the sake of this last step that we did not eagerly simplify using the one-point rule with
(i,i^{\prime})=(n,n^{\prime})
.
For the quantifier-free notation to make sense, everything needs to be a predicate of the same type.)

All the other cases are proved similarly.

Encoding relational VCs in terms of store relations.

The unary VCs are given in terms of store predicates (
Figure 7
).
For relational VCs, although the annotation comprises store relations,
the relational VCs involve alignment conditions (
L,R,J
) which are sets of
alignment automaton states.
So the relational VCs are given in terms of states, using
\hat{an}

and
\breve{an}
in Figs.
8
and
9
.
For use in RHL+ proofs about programs in automaton normal form,
we will encode the VCs in terms of relations on stores that use
a variable
pc
(for “program counter”) to encode control information.
(The normal form is sketched in
section 2
and developed in
section 5
.)

We often use the following abbreviations for assignments and tests of
the chosen
pc
variable.

\mathord{!}n
for
pc:=n

\mathord{?}n
for
pc=n

for literal
n\in{\rm Nature}

(9)

To be precise, we define
\mathord{!}n
to be
pc:=^{0}n
, using
0
arbitrarily as the label. Our uses of
\mathord{!}n
will be in contexts where labels are irrelevant,
in commands that are not required to be
ok
.
Note that the notations
\mathord{!}n
and
\mathord{?}n
depend, implicitly, on the choice of the variable
pc
.

{defi}
Let
R
be a set of states of a program alignment automaton.
Let
pc
be a variable such that
\mbox{{indep}}(pc|pc,R)
.
Define the
pc
-encoded
R
to be a relation on stores as follows:

\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$\tilde{R}$}\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\{(s,s^{\prime})\mid((s(pc),s^{\prime}(pc)),(s,s^{\prime}))\in R\}
.

Lemma 11
.

Let
R
be set of alignment automaton states such that
\mbox{{indep}}(pc|pc,R)
.
If
(s,s^{\prime})\in\tilde{R}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}\mathord{?}n\mathbin{\mid}\mathord{?}n^{\prime}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }

then
((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in R
.

Proof 4.3
.

Suppose
(s,s^{\prime})\in\tilde{R}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}\mathord{?}n\mathbin{\mid}\mathord{?}n^{\prime}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }
.
Then
s(pc)=n
and
s^{\prime}(pc)=n^{\prime}

by definition of
\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}\mathord{?}n\mathbin{\mid}\mathord{?}n^{\prime}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }
,
hence
((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in R
by definition of
\tilde{R}
.

The encoding makes it possible to derive
pc
-encoded forms of the verification conditions in terms of store relations, enabling their use in deductive proofs.

if
\mbox{{sub}}(n,c)
is…

and
m
is…

then the encoded VC for
((n,n^{\prime}),(m,n^{\prime}))
is
\ldots

\mathsf{skip}^{n}

\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,c,f)

\tilde{L}\land\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}\mathord{?}n\mathbin{\mid}\mathord{?}n^{\prime}\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }\land an(n,n^{\prime})\Rightarrow an(m,n^{\prime})

x:=^{n}e

\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,c,f)

\tilde{L}\land\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}\mathord{?}n\mathbin{\mid}\mathord{?}n^{\prime}\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }\land an(n,n^{\prime})\Rightarrow{an(m,n^{\prime})}^{x\mbox{\tiny$|$}}_{e\mbox{\tiny$|$}}

\mathsf{hav}^{n}\ {x}

\mbox{{fsuc}}(n,c,f)

\tilde{L}\land\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}\mathord{?}n\mathbin{\mid}\mathord{?}n^{\prime}\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }\land an(n,n^{\prime})\Rightarrow\dot{\forall}x\mbox{\tiny$|$}.\>an(m,n^{\prime})

\mathsf{if}^{n}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{fi}

Lemma 12

(pc-encoded VCs for alignment automata)
.

Let
an
be an annotation of an alignment automaton

\prod(\mbox{{aut}}(c,f),\mbox{{aut}}(c^{\prime},f^{\prime}),L,R,J)
for commands
c,c^{\prime}
.
Suppose
pc
is a fresh variable in the sense that
it does not occur in
c
or
c^{\prime}
, and all of
L,R,J
and any
an(i,j)
are independent from

pc
on both sides.
Then each left-only relational VC of
Figure 8
implies
the corresponding condition on store relations in
Figure 10
.
Each joint relational VC in

Figure 9
implies a corresponding condition (see
Figure 11
).
Similarly for right-only VCs.

Proof 4.4
.

Consider the left-only skip case.
The primary VC is
L\land\breve{an}(n,n^{\prime})\Rightarrow\hat{an}(m,n^{\prime})

(first row of
Figure 8
),
which abbreviates

L\land[n|n^{\prime}]\land\hat{an}(n,n^{\prime})\Rightarrow\hat{an}(m,n^{\prime})

(10)

We show this implication entails
the first line in
Figure 10
, i.e.,

\tilde{L}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}\mathord{?}n\mathbin{\mid}\mathord{?}n^{\prime}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }\land an(n,n^{\prime})\Rightarrow an(m,n^{\prime})
.
To prove the latter implication, observe for any
(s,s^{\prime})

\begin{array}[]{lll}&(s,s^{\prime})\in\tilde{L}\land(s,s^{\prime})\in\raisebox{0.96873pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}\mathord{?}n\mathbin{\mid}\mathord{?}n^{\prime}\raisebox{0.96873pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }\land(s,s^{\prime})\in an(n,n^{\prime})\\
\Rightarrow&((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in L\land(s,s^{\prime})\in an(n,n^{\prime})&\mbox{Lemma~\ref{lem:state-to-store}}\\
\Leftrightarrow&((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in L\land((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in[n|n^{\prime}]\land(s,s^{\prime})\in an(n,n^{\prime})&\mbox{def $[n|n^{\prime}]$}\\
\Leftrightarrow&((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in L\land((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in[n|n^{\prime}]\land((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in\hat{an}(n,n^{\prime})&\mbox{def (\ref{eq:hatALT})}\\
\Rightarrow&((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in\hat{an}(m,n^{\prime})&\mbox{primary VC (\ref{eq:primVC}) }\\
\Leftrightarrow&(s,s^{\prime})\in an(m,n^{\prime})&\mbox{def (\ref{eq:hatALT})}\\
\end{array}

The argument is essentially the same for all cases in Figs.
8

and
9
.

5.
Automaton normal form reduction and KAT

5.1.
Command equivalence and KAT

{defi}

A
Kleene algebra with tests

[
Koz97
]
(
KAT
) is a structure
(\mathbb{K},\mathbb{B},+,\mathbin{;},*,\mbox{$\lnot$},1,0)

such that
(a)
\mathbb{K}
is a set and
\mathbb{B}\subseteq\mathbb{K}
(elements of
\mathbb{B}
are called
tests
).
(b)
\mathbb{B}
contains
1
and
0
, and is closed under the operations
+,\mathbin{;},\mbox{$\lnot$}
,
and these satisfy the laws of Boolean algebra, with
1
as true
and
\mathbin{;}
as conjunction.
(c)
\mathbb{K}
is an idempotent semiring and the
following hold for all
x,y,z
in
\mathbb{K}
.

\begin{array}[]{lcl@{\hspace{5em}}lcl}1+x\mathbin{;}x^{*}&=&x^{*}\hfil\hskip 50.00008pt&y+x\mathbin{;}z\leq z&\Rightarrow&x^{*}\mathbin{;}y\leq z\\
1+x^{*}\mathbin{;}x&=&x^{*}\hfil\hskip 50.00008pt&y+z\mathbin{;}x\leq z&\Rightarrow&y\mathbin{;}x^{*}\leq z\end{array}

The ordering
\leq
is defined by
x\leq y
iff
x+y=y
.
The operator
;
binds tighter than
+
.

In a
relational model

[
KS96
]
,
\mathbb{K}
is some set of relations on some set
\Sigma
,
with
0
the empty relation,

1
the identity relation on
\Sigma
,

+
union of relations,

\mathbin{;}
relational composition, and
*
reflexive-transitive closure.
Moreover
\mathbb{B}
is a set of
coreflexives
, i.e., subsets of the identity relation
1
,
and
\lnot
is complement with respect to
1
.
In a relational model,
\leq
is set inclusion.
The
relational model for GCL
, denoted
\mathfrak{L}
, is the relational model comprising all relations on
\Sigma
where
\Sigma
is the set of variable stores.
15
15

15

KAT is complete for relational models
and there are other completeness results for KAT
[
KS96
]
. We make no use of those results. All we need about KAT is that it is sufficient for our
21
. That result is like

[
Koz97
]
which uses KAT to prove that every command is simulated by one with a single loop.

Representing programs in KAT

In this paper we work with
\mathfrak{L}
and also with equational hypotheses formalized in terms of KAT expressions which are usually defined with respect to given finite sets of primitive tests and actions
[
KS96
, Sect. 2.3]
.
We only need the special case where the actions are primitive commands and the tests are primitive boolean expressions.
16
16

16

Unlike in
[
KS96
]
, we have infinitely many primitives,
because the GCL grammar generates infinitely many boolean and arithemetic expressions, but this causes no problems.

Define the
KAT expressions
as follows.
17
17

17

The primitives
\underline{bprim}
,
\underline{x:=e}
, and
\underline{\mathsf{hav}\ {x}}
are written this way to avoid confusion with their counterparts in GCL syntax.

Recall that
bprim
stands for primitive boolean expressions in GCL.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{KB}&::=&\underline{bprim}\mid\mathit{KB}+\mathit{KB}\mid\mathit{KB}\mathbin{;}\mathit{KB}\mid\mbox{$\lnot$}\mathit{KB}\mid 1\mid 0\\
\mathit{KE}&::=&\underline{x:=e}\mid\underline{\mathsf{hav}\ {x}}\mid\mathit{KE}+\mathit{KE}\mid\mathit{KE}\mathbin{;}\mathit{KE}\mid\mathit{KE}^{*}\mid\mathit{KB}\end{array}

We refer to terms given by
\mathit{KB}
as KAT boolean expressions and by
\mathit{KE}
as KAT expressions.

Given any model, and mappings of the primitive expressions to elements in the model, one obtains an interpretation of all KAT expressions in the model
[
KS96
, Sect. 2.3]
.
To be precise, let
\mathfrak{M}
be a model
(\mathbb{K}^{\mathfrak{M}},\mathbb{B}^{\mathfrak{M}},+^{\mathfrak{M}},\mathbin{;}^{\mathfrak{M}},*^{\mathfrak{M}},\mbox{$\lnot$}^{\mathfrak{M}},1^{\mathfrak{M}},0^{\mathfrak{M}})
.
Given interpretations
\underline{bprim}^{\mathfrak{M}}
,
\underline{x:=e}^{\mathfrak{M}}
,
and
\underline{\mathsf{hav}\ {x}}^{\mathfrak{M}}

in
\mathfrak{M}
for each primitive KAT expression,
we get an interpretation
\mathit{KE}^{\mathfrak{M}}
for every
\mathit{KE}
,
defined homomorphically as usual.
For example:

(\mathit{KE}_{0}+\mathit{KE}_{1})^{\mathfrak{M}}\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\mathit{KE}_{0}^{\mathfrak{M}}\,+^{\mathfrak{M}}\,\mathit{KE}_{1}^{\mathfrak{M}}
.
Henceforth we never write
+^{\mathfrak{M}}
because the reader can infer whether the symbol

+
is meant as syntax in a KAT expression or as an operation of a particular KAT.

The
GCL-to-KAT translation

\llparenthesis\,-\,\rrparenthesis

maps GCL commands (resp. boolean expressions) to KAT expressions (
\mathit{KE}
)
(resp. KAT boolean expressions (
\mathit{KB}
)).
For boolean expressions it is defined by structural recursion as follows:

\llparenthesis\,bprim\,\rrparenthesis\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\underline{bprim}\qquad\llparenthesis\,\mbox{$\lnot$}e\,\rrparenthesis\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\mbox{$\lnot$}\llparenthesis\,e\,\rrparenthesis\qquad\llparenthesis\,e\land e^{\prime}\,\rrparenthesis\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\llparenthesis\,e\,\rrparenthesis\mathbin{;}\llparenthesis\,e^{\prime}\,\rrparenthesis\qquad\llparenthesis\,e\lor e^{\prime}\,\rrparenthesis\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\llparenthesis\,e\,\rrparenthesis+\llparenthesis\,e^{\prime}\,\rrparenthesis

For commands the translation is defined by mutual recursion with the definition of
\llparenthesis\,gcs\,\rrparenthesis
:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\;}c@{\;}l@{\hspace*{3em}}l@{\;}c@{\;}l}\llparenthesis\,x:=e\,\rrparenthesis&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\underline{x:=e}\hfil\hskip 27.74982pt&\llparenthesis\,c;d\,\rrparenthesis&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\llparenthesis\,c\,\rrparenthesis\mathbin{;}\llparenthesis\,d\,\rrparenthesis\\
\llparenthesis\,\mathsf{hav}\ {x}\,\rrparenthesis&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\underline{\mathsf{hav}\ {x}}\hfil\hskip 27.74982pt&\llparenthesis\,\mathsf{if}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{fi}\,\rrparenthesis&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\llparenthesis\,gcs\,\rrparenthesis\\
\llparenthesis\,\mathsf{skip}\,\rrparenthesis&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&1\hfil\hskip 27.74982pt&\llparenthesis\,\mathsf{do}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od}\,\rrparenthesis&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\llparenthesis\,gcs\,\rrparenthesis^{*}\mathbin{;}\mbox{$\lnot$}\llparenthesis\,\mbox{{enab}}(gcs)\,\rrparenthesis\\[3.87498pt]
\llparenthesis\,gcs\,\rrparenthesis&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\lx@intercol(+\,e,c\>:\>(e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c)\in gcs\>:\>\llparenthesis\,e\,\rrparenthesis\mathbin{;}\llparenthesis\,c\,\rrparenthesis)\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}

(11)

This is an adaptation of the well known translation of imperative programs into KAT
[
Koz97
]
.

We define interpretations in
\mathfrak{L}
for the primitive expressions, as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lclllcl}\underline{x:=e}^{\mathfrak{L}}&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\llbracket\,x:=e\,\rrbracket&&\underline{bprim}^{\mathfrak{L}}&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\{(s,s)\mid\llbracket\,bprim\,\rrbracket(s)=true\}\\
\underline{\mathsf{hav}\ {x}}^{\mathfrak{L}}&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\llbracket\,\mathsf{hav}\ {x}\,\rrbracket\end{array}

As with an interpretation in any model, this induces an interpretation

\mathit{KE}^{\mathfrak{L}}
for any KAT expression
\mathit{KE}
.

Lemma 13
.

For all commands
c
we have
\llparenthesis\,c\,\rrparenthesis^{\mathfrak{L}}=\llbracket\,c\,\rrbracket
.
For all boolean expressions
e
we have

\llparenthesis\,e\,\rrparenthesis^{\mathfrak{L}}=\{(s,s)\mid\llbracket\,e\,\rrbracket(s)=true\}
.

Proof 5.1
.

The proof is by induction on
e
and then on
c
.
The base cases are by definition.
A secondary induction is used for the loop case.

Command equivalence and soundness of HL+ and RHL+.

For specifications where the pre- and post-condition are expressible as tests in the KAT,
one can express correctness judgments
[
Koz00
]
.
In our setting, for boolean expressions
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
we have that

\models c:e_{0}\leadsto e_{1}
is equivalent to the equation

\llparenthesis\,e_{0}\,\rrparenthesis\mathbin{;}\llparenthesis\,c\,\rrparenthesis\mathbin{;}\mbox{$\lnot$}\llparenthesis\,e_{1}\,\rrparenthesis=0
being true in
\mathfrak{L}
.
In this paper we do not use the KAT formulation for correctness judgments in general, but we do use it in a limited way.

Let
H
be a set of equations between KAT expressions.
We write
H\vdash\mathit{KE}_{0}=\mathit{KE}_{1}
to say
\mathit{KE}_{0}=\mathit{KE}_{1}

is provable by equational reasoning from hypotheses
H
plus
the axioms of KAT (
subsection 5.1
).

The idea for the equivalence condition
c\mathrel{\simeq}d
in rules
Rewrite
(
Figure 1
) and
rRewrite
(
Figure 2
)
is that it should mean
H\vdash\llparenthesis\,c\,\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis\,d\,\rrparenthesis
for a suitable set of hypotheses
that axiomatize the semantics of some primitive boolean expressions and commands.
To prove our main results we only need a few axioms (as detailed in
Appendix B
).
For the sake of a straightforward presentation we
formulate equivalence in terms of a larger set of axioms.

{defi}

Define
Hyp
to be the set of equations given by:
(a) the equation
\llparenthesis\,e\,\rrparenthesis=0
for every boolean expression
e

such that
e\Rightarrow\mathit{false}
is valid;
(b) the equation
\llparenthesis\,e_{0}\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,x:=e\,\rrparenthesis;\mbox{$\lnot$}\llparenthesis\,e_{1}\,\rrparenthesis=0

for all assignments
x:=e

and boolean expressions
e_{0}
,
e_{1}

such that
e_{0}\Rightarrow{e_{1}}^{x}_{e}
is valid;
(c) the equation

\llparenthesis\,e_{0}\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathsf{hav}\ {x}\,\rrparenthesis;\mbox{$\lnot$}\llparenthesis\,e_{1}\,\rrparenthesis=0

for
x,e_{0},e_{1}
such that
e_{0}\Rightarrow\dot{\forall}x.\>e_{1}
is valid.

{defi}

[command equivalence]
Define
\mathrel{\simeq}
by

\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$c\mathrel{\simeq}d$}\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\mbox{{Hyp}}\vdash\llparenthesis\,c\,\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis\,d\,\rrparenthesis
.

An example is the equivalence

\mathsf{do}\ {e_{0}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c}\ \mathsf{od}\;\mathrel{\simeq}\;\mathsf{do}\ {e_{0}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c;\mathsf{do}\ {e_{0}\land e_{1}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c}\ \mathsf{od}}\ \mathsf{od}

which holds for any
c,e_{0},e_{1}
. It is used in the loop tiling example,
see (
1
).

Remark 14
.

It is straightforward to present a deductive system for
\mathrel{\simeq}
,
based on
Hyp
, the axioms of KAT, and the rules of equational logic.
For practical purposes an alternative is to leverage the fact that the hypotheses are all equations of the form
\mathit{KE}=0
. For any finite set
H
of such equations, entailments
H\vdash\mathit{KE}_{0}=\mathit{KE}_{1}
are decidable in PSPACE
[
CKS96
]
.
Many practical cases of
\mathrel{\simeq}
require no hypotheses at all. For the equivalences used in our normal form theorem, the requisite hypotheses are a finite set
of KAT-consequences of
Hyp
,
syntactically determined by the relevant command
c
as detailed in

Appendix B
.
∎

Having defined
\mathrel{\simeq}
,
the key ingredient of the rules
Rewrite
and
rRewrite
,
we have completed the definition of HL+ and RHL+.

Lemma 15
.

Every equation in
Hyp
holds in
\mathfrak{L}
.

This is an easy consequence of the definition of
Hyp
.

Theorem 16
.

All the rules of HL+ are sound.

Proof 5.2
.

The proofs are straightforward using the definitions, and induction for the loop rule.
The only rule which is not standard is
Rewrite
which we prove as follows.
Suppose
c\mathrel{\simeq}d
holds.
That means
\mbox{{Hyp}}\vdash\llparenthesis\,c\,\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis\,d\,\rrparenthesis
so

\llparenthesis\,c\,\rrparenthesis^{\mathfrak{M}}=\llparenthesis\,d\,\rrparenthesis^{\mathfrak{M}}
in any model
\mathfrak{M}
that satisfies
the equations
Hyp
.
So by
15
we have
\llparenthesis\,c\,\rrparenthesis^{\mathfrak{L}}=\llparenthesis\,d\,\rrparenthesis^{\mathfrak{L}}
.
Hence
\llbracket\,c\,\rrbracket=\llbracket\,d\,\rrbracket
by
13
.
Suppose the premise of
Rewrite
holds,
i.e.,
\models c:P\leadsto Q
.
This is a condition on
\llbracket\,c\,\rrbracket

—see (
2
)—
so we have
\models d:P\leadsto Q
.

Theorem 17
.

All the rules of RHL+ (
Figure 2
) are sound.

Proof 5.3
.

The proofs are straightforward using the definitions, and induction for the loop rule.
(Similar loop rules are proved sound in
[
Ber11
,
BNNN22
]
and in the long version of
[
NN21
]
.)
The proof of
rRewrite
is similar to the proof of
Rewrite
for
16
.

5.2.
Automaton normal form

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(\mathsf{skip}^{n})&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\mathord{!}n\,;\mathsf{skip}^{n}\\
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(x:=^{n}e)&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\mathord{!}n\,;x:=^{n}e\\
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(\mathsf{hav}^{n}\ {x})&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\mathord{!}n\,;\mathsf{hav}^{n}\ {x}\\
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(c;d)&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(c)\,;\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(d)\\
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(\mathsf{if}^{n}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{fi})&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\mathord{!}n\,;\mathsf{if}^{n}\ {(\mbox{{add}}^{pc}_{0}(gcs))}\ \mathsf{fi}\\
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(\mathsf{do}^{n}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od})&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&\mathord{!}n\,;\mathsf{do}^{n}\ {(\mbox{{add}}^{pc}_{1}(n,gcs))}\ \mathsf{od}\\[3.44444pt]
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}_{0}(e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c)&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(c)\\[1.72221pt]
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}_{0}(e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c\talloblong gcs)&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(c)\talloblong\mbox{{add}}^{pc}_{0}(gcs)\\[3.44444pt]
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}_{1}(n,e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c)&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(c);\mathord{!}n\\[1.72221pt]
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}_{1}(n,e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c\talloblong gcs)&\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}&e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(c);\mathord{!}n\talloblong\mbox{{add}}^{pc}_{1}(n,gcs)\end{array}

Figure 12.
Definition of
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}
.

Choose a variable name
pc
.

Figure 12
defines
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}
, a function from commands to commands
that adds
pc
as in the example
c0^{+}
on page
2
.
The definition of
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}
is by structural recursion, with mutually recursive
helpers
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}_{0}
and
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}_{1}
, and is written using the
abbreviations in (
9
).
For
\mathsf{if}
commands,
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}_{0}
maps
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}
over the guarded commands,
and for
\mathsf{do}
,
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}_{1}
additionally adds a trailing assignment to set
pc
to
the loop label.

Lemma 18
.

If
pc
does not occur in
c
then
\mbox{{erase}}(pc,\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(c))\mathrel{\simeq}c

and
\mbox{{ghost}}(pc,\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(c))
holds.

Proof 5.4
.

To show
\mbox{{erase}}(pc,\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(c))\mathrel{\simeq}c
,
we must show
\mbox{{Hyp}}\vdash\llparenthesis\,\mbox{{erase}}(pc,\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(c))\,\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis\,c\,\rrparenthesis
.
In fact we can show
\vdash\llparenthesis\,\mbox{{erase}}(pc,\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(c))\,\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis\,c\,\rrparenthesis
.
To do so, go by induction on
c
, using that
\llparenthesis\,\mathsf{skip}\,\rrparenthesis=1

and
1
is the unit of sequence.
The proof of
\mbox{{ghost}}(pc,\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(c))
is also a straightforward induction on
c
.

\frac{}{\mathsf{skip}^{n}\mathbin{/}f\hookrightarrow(\mathord{?}n\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathord{!}f)}\par\frac{}{x:=^{n}e\mathbin{/}f\hookrightarrow(\mathord{?}n\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=e;\mathord{!}f)}\par\frac{}{\mathsf{hav}^{n}\ {x}\mathbin{/}f\hookrightarrow(\mathord{?}n\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathsf{hav}\ {x};\mathord{!}f)}\par\frac{c_{0}\mathbin{/}f\hookrightarrow gcs_{0}\hskip 8.50012ptc_{1}\mathbin{/}f\hookrightarrow gcs_{1}}{\mathsf{if}^{n}\ {e_{0}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c_{0}\talloblong e_{1}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c_{1}}\ \mathsf{fi}\mathbin{/}f\hookrightarrow\mathord{?}n\land e_{0}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathord{!}\mbox{{lab}}(c_{0})\talloblong\mathord{?}n\land e_{1}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathord{!}\mbox{{lab}}(c_{1})\talloblong gcs_{0}\talloblong gcs_{1}}\par\frac{c\mathbin{/}\mbox{{lab}}(d)\hookrightarrow gcs_{0}\hskip 8.50012ptd\mathbin{/}f\hookrightarrow gcs_{1}}{c;d\mathbin{/}f\hookrightarrow gcs_{0}\talloblong gcs_{1}}\par\frac{c\mathbin{/}n\hookrightarrow gcs}{\mathsf{do}^{n}\ {e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c}\ \mathsf{od}\mathbin{/}f\hookrightarrow\mathord{?}n\land e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathord{!}\mbox{{lab}}(c)\talloblong\mathord{?}n\land\mbox{$\lnot$}e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathord{!}f\talloblong gcs}\par\frac{c_{0}\mathbin{/}n\hookrightarrow gcs_{0}\hskip 8.50012ptc_{1}\mathbin{/}n\hookrightarrow gcs_{1}}{\mathsf{do}^{n}\ {e_{0}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c_{0}\talloblong e_{1}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c_{1}}\ \mathsf{od}\mathbin{/}f\hookrightarrow\mathord{?}n\land e_{0}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathord{!}\mbox{{lab}}(c_{0})\talloblong\mathord{?}n\land e_{1}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathord{!}\mbox{{lab}}(c_{1})\talloblong\mathord{?}n\land\mbox{$\lnot$}(e_{0}\lor e_{1})\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathord{!}f\talloblong gcs_{0}\talloblong gcs_{1}}\par

Figure 13.
Normal form bodies.

Figure 13
defines the ternary relation
c\mathbin{/}m\hookrightarrow gcs

to relate a command and a label to the gcs that will be the body of its normal form.
To be precise, the relation depends on a choice of
pc
variable but we leave this implicit, as it is in the notations
\mathord{?}\_
and
\mathord{!}\_
of (
9
).
We can write “
c\mathbin{/}m\hookrightarrow gcs
(for
pc
)” to make the choice explicit.
For readability,
Figure 13
gives special cases for if and do.
The general form is relegated to
Appendix C
.

An example is
x:=^{4}x-1\mathbin{/}2\hookrightarrow(\mathord{?}4\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=x-1;\mathord{!}2)
.
For the running example, we have
c0\mathbin{/}6\hookrightarrow gcs
where
gcs
is the body of example
d0
on page
2
.

There are six kinds of transition in the small step semantics.
Each kind has a corresponding form of guarded command in the normal form.
We spell them out for later reference.

Lemma 19

(guarded commands of a normal form)
.

Suppose
\mbox{{okf}}(c,f)
and
c\mathbin{/}f\hookrightarrow gcs
.
Every guarded command in
gcs
has one of these six forms:

•

\mathord{?}k\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathord{!}m
,
for some
k,m
such that
\mbox{{sub}}(k,c)
is
\mathsf{skip}^{k}
and
m=\mbox{{fsuc}}(k,c,f)
.

•

\mathord{?}k\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=e;\mathord{!}m
,
for some
k,m,x,e
such that
\mbox{{sub}}(k,c)
is
x:=^{k}e

and
m=\mbox{{fsuc}}(k,c,f)
.

•

\mathord{?}k\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathsf{hav}\ {x};\mathord{!}m
,
for some
k,m,x
such that
\mbox{{sub}}(k,c)
is
\mathsf{hav}^{k}\ {x}

and
m=\mbox{{fsuc}}(k,c,f)
.

•

\mathord{?}k\land e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathord{!}m
,
for some
k,m,e,d,gcs_{0}
such that
\mbox{{sub}}(k,c)
is
\mathsf{if}^{k}\ {gcs_{0}}\ \mathsf{fi}

and
m=\mbox{{lab}}(d)
where
e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}d
is in
gcs_{0}
.

•

\mathord{?}k\land e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathord{!}m
,
for some
k,m,e,d,gcs_{0}
such that
\mbox{{sub}}(k,c)
is
\mathsf{do}^{k}\ {gcs_{0}}\ \mathsf{od}

and
m=\mbox{{lab}}(d)
where
e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}d
is in
gcs_{0}
.

•

\mathord{?}k\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\mbox{{enab}}(gcs_{0})\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathord{!}m
,
for some
k,m,gcs_{0}
such that
\mbox{{sub}}(k,c)
is

\mathsf{do}^{k}\ {gcs_{0}}\ \mathsf{od}
and
m=\mbox{{fsuc}}(k,c,f)
.

Lemma 20
.

For all
c
and
f
, there is some
gcs
with
c\mathbin{/}f\hookrightarrow gcs
.

Proof 5.5
.

Straightforward structural induction on
c
.

In fact
gcs
is uniquely determined by
c
,
m
, and the chosen variable
pc
. But none of our results depend on uniqueness.

{defi}

An
automaton normal form
of a command
c
with
\mbox{{lab}}(c)=n
and label
f\notin\mbox{{labs}}(c)
, for chosen variable
pc
, is
the command

\>\mathord{!}n;\mathsf{do}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od}\>

where
c\mathbin{/}f\hookrightarrow gcs
.

This does not require
c
to be
ok
, but the normal form is only useful if
\mbox{{okf}}(c,f)
.

As noted in
section 1
, we are not using the term “normal form”
in the sense of term rewriting systems
[
BN99
]
.
It is not the case that semantically equal programs have
identical automaton normal forms.
The important property is the theorem to follow.

Normal form equivalence theorem.

Finally we are ready for the main result of
section 5
,
which loosely speaking says every command is equivalent to one in automaton normal form.

Theorem 21
.

If
\mbox{{okf}}(c,f)
,
pc\notin vars(c)
, and

c\mathbin{/}f\hookrightarrow gcs
(for
pc
)
then

\mathord{!}n;\mathsf{do}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od}\mathrel{\simeq}\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(c);\mathord{!}f\quad\mbox{where $n=\mbox{{lab}}(c)$.}

(12)

Proof 5.6
.

By definition of
\mathrel{\simeq}
we must prove

\mbox{{Hyp}}\vdash\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}n;\mathsf{do}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od}\,\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis\,\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(c);\mathord{!}f\,\rrparenthesis

using laws of KAT.
In fact we do not need all of
Hyp
; the proof shows that

\mbox{{nfax}}(pc,c,f)\vdash\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}n;\mathsf{do}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od}\,\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis\,\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(c);\mathord{!}f\,\rrparenthesis

where
\mbox{{nfax}}(pc,c,f)
is the relevant finite set of axioms for
c

(detailed in
Appendix B
).
18
18

18

As remarked following
Def.
5.1
, owing to the form of our hypotheses any instance
is decidable (in PSPACE). But this does not help prove the theorem,
where we have infinitely many instances to prove.

These include the following:

(setTest)

\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}i\,\rrparenthesis\mathbin{;}\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}i\,\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}i\,\rrparenthesis
for
i
in
\mbox{{labs}}(c)\cup\{f\}
.

(diffTest)

\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}i\,\rrparenthesis\mathbin{;}\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}j\,\rrparenthesis=0
for

i
and
j
in
\mbox{{labs}}(c)\cup\{f\}
such that
i\neq j
.

By equational reasoning these yield consequences such as

(diffTestNeg)

\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}i\,\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}i\,\rrparenthesis;\mbox{$\lnot$}\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}j\,\rrparenthesis

for
i\neq j
with
i,j
in
\mbox{{labs}}(c)\cup\{f\}
.

(nf-enab-labs)

\llparenthesis\,\mbox{{enab}}(gcs)\,\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis\,(\lor\,i\>:\>i\in\mbox{{labs}}(c)\>:\>\mathord{?}i)\,\rrparenthesis
.

The theorem’s proof goes by rule induction on
c\mathbin{/}f\hookrightarrow gcs
.
There is one normal form rule per command form, so we proceed by cases on those forms.
In each case, aside from unfolding definitions of
\mbox{{add}}^{pc}
,
\llparenthesis\,-\,\rrparenthesis
, etc.,
we use only KAT reasoning and the
nfax
hypotheses,
together with induction hypotheses for subprograms.
The lengthy details can be found in
[
NBN23
, Appendix D]
; here we show just one case.

Case
c
is
x:=^{n}e
.
We have
x:=^{n}e\mathbin{/}f\hookrightarrow(\mathord{?}n\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=^{n}e;\mathord{!}f)
.
Operationally, the loop
\mathsf{do}\ {\mathord{?}n\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=^{n}e;\mathord{!}f}\ \mathsf{od}

iterates exactly once. This is reflected in our proof of (
12
) for this case, in which we unroll the loop once;
see the calculation in
Figure 14
.
Note: in hints we do not mention associativity, unit law for 1, etc.

\begin{array}[]{lll}&\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}n;\mathsf{do}\ {\mathord{?}n\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=^{n}e;\mathord{!}f}\ \mathsf{od}\,\rrparenthesis\\
=&{\qquad\mbox{def $\llparenthesis\,-\,\rrparenthesis$, see (\ref{eq:def:mkt}), and def $\mbox{{enab}}$ }}\\
&\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}n\,\rrparenthesis;(\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}n\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,x:=^{n}e\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}f\,\rrparenthesis)^{*};\mbox{$\lnot$}\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}n\,\rrparenthesis\\
=&{\qquad\mbox{star unfold, distrib}}\\
&\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}n\,\rrparenthesis;\mbox{$\lnot$}\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}n\,\rrparenthesis+\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}n\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}n\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,x:=^{n}e\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}f\,\rrparenthesis;(\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}n\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,x:=^{n}e\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}f\,\rrparenthesis)^{*};\mbox{$\lnot$}\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}n\,\rrparenthesis\\
=&{\qquad\mbox{left term is 0, using axiom (setTest) and lemma (diffTestNeg)}}\\
&\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}n\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}n\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,x:=^{n}e\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}f\,\rrparenthesis;(\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}n\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,x:=^{n}e\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}f\,\rrparenthesis)^{*};\mbox{$\lnot$}\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}n\,\rrparenthesis\\
=&{\qquad\mbox{(setTest) for $n$}}\\
&\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}n\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,x:=^{n}e\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}f\,\rrparenthesis;(\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}n\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,x:=^{n}e\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}f\,\rrparenthesis)^{*};\mbox{$\lnot$}\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}n\,\rrparenthesis\\
=&{\qquad\mbox{(setTest) for $f$}}\\
&\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}n\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,x:=^{n}e\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}f\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}f\,\rrparenthesis;(\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}n\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,x:=^{n}e\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}f\,\rrparenthesis)^{*};\mbox{$\lnot$}\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}n\,\rrparenthesis\\
=&{\qquad\mbox{(diffTest) with $f\neq n$ from $\mbox{{okf}}(c,f)$; KAT fact $p;(q;a)^{*}=p$ if $p;q=0$ (any $p,q,a$) }}\\
&\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}n\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,x:=^{n}e\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}f\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}f\,\rrparenthesis;\mbox{$\lnot$}\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}n\,\rrparenthesis\\
=&{\qquad\mbox{(diffTestNeg), $f\neq n$ by $\mbox{{okf}}(c,f)$}}\\
&\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}n\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,x:=^{n}e\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}f\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{?}f\,\rrparenthesis\\
=&{\qquad\mbox{(setTest)}}\\
&\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}n\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,x:=^{n}e\,\rrparenthesis;\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}f\,\rrparenthesis\\
=&{\qquad\mbox{def $\llparenthesis\,-\,\rrparenthesis$}}\\
&\llparenthesis\,\mathord{!}n;x:=^{n}e;\mathord{!}f\,\rrparenthesis\\
=&{\qquad\mbox{def $\mbox{{add}}^{pc}$}}\\
&\llparenthesis\,\mbox{{add}}^{pc}(x:=^{n}e);\mathord{!}f\,\rrparenthesis\end{array}
