{gcs}\ \mathsf{od}\mathbin{\>\mid\>}\mathsf{do}\ {gcs^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{od}:\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{Q}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}\mbox{{enab}}(gcs)\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}\mbox{{enab}}(gcs^{\prime})\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{eDo}

Figure 15.
Rules of ERHL+.
In
eDo
,
(D,\prec)
is well-ordered and

V
is a total function
(\mbox{{Var}}\to\mathbb{Z})\times(\mbox{{Var}}\to\mathbb{Z})\to D
.
Rules
eRewrite
,
eGhost
,
eSkip
,
eSkipAss
,
eAssSkip
,
eSeq
,
eIf
,
eConseq
,
eDisj
, and
eFalse
are the same as those with corresponding names in
Figure 2
but for
\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}
.

The rules of ERHL+ appear in
Figure 15
. Most have the same form as corresponding rules in
Figure 2
,
although the soundness proofs are different in detail.
If
d
is deterministic then
\models c\mathbin{\mid}d:\mathcal{P}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{Q}
implies

\models c\mathbin{\mid}d:\mathcal{P}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{Q}
. If
d
denotes a domain-total
23
23

23

Domain-totality means the command may terminate from any state, not that it must.
For example,
\mathsf{if}\ {true\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}diverge\talloblong true\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathsf{skip}}\ \mathsf{fi}
is domain-total.

relation then

\models c\mathbin{\mid}d:\mathcal{P}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{Q}
implies
\models c\mathbin{\mid}d:\mathcal{P}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{Q}
. This hints at
why some proof rules for
\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}
are the same as those in

Figure 2
for the
\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}
judgment.

The rule
eSkipHav
reflects the
\forall\exists
nature of the
\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}
judgment,
existentially quantifying
x
on the right state
whereas
eHavSkip
universally quantifies it on the left.
Note that
eIf
and
eDo
do
not
existentially quantify over guarded commands on the right, as one might guess at first. This would be unsound, because property (
3
) universally quantifies over all pairs of initial states; we return to this later.
A distinguishing feature of the
\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}
judgment is that it does not validate the rule of conjunction which is sound for the
\forall\forall
judgment. For example,
both
\mathsf{skip}\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{hav}\ {x}:true\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}x<0\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
and

\mathsf{skip}\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{hav}\ {x}:true\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}x>0\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
are valid
but not
\mathsf{skip}\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{hav}\ {x}:true\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}x<0\land x>0\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
.

Besides
eSkipHav
, another noticeable difference from RHL+ is
eDo
.
Like the rule
rDo
in RHL+,
eDo
captures a conditional alignment of iterations directed by the
relations
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}
. The side condition connects the invariant
\mathcal{Q}
with
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}
in a way that ensures adequacy in the sense of covering all iterations. The rule differs
from
rDo
in its treatment of right-only iterations. The
\forall\exists

judgment requires termination on the right, and the rule relies on the standard
approach of showing a variant decreases.
24
24

24

As an alternative to
universally quantifying over
v\in D
in the metalanguage, it is possible to
formulate rule
eDo
using a fresh program variable to snapshot the initial value of
V
, with side condition
\mathcal{Q}\Rightarrow V\geq 0
.
This is sufficient because for completeness it suffices to take
D
to
be
{\rm Nature}
; this is shown in the proof of
31
.

The variant
V
maps pairs of stores to some well-ordered set
D
.
In accord with the shallow embedding of
relational assertions, for any value
v
in
D
we write
V=v
to denote the
relation
\{(s,s^{\prime})\mid V(s,s^{\prime})=v\}
.
Likewise for
V\prec v
.

Formally the premise for
\mathsf{skip}\mathbin{\mid}c^{\prime}
in
eDo
is a
D
-indexed set of premises,
but this is just an artifact of shallow embedding.
Think of
v
as a logical variable in a single premise,
universally quantified over the judgment.

\frac{}{\mathsf{hav}\ {x}\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{hav}\ {x^{\prime}}:(\dot{\forall}x|.\>(\dot{\exists}|x^{\prime}.\>\mathcal{P}))\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{P}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{eHavHav}\par\frac{}{x:=e\mathbin{\mid}x^{\prime}:=e^{\prime}:{\mathcal{R}}^{x|x^{\prime}}_{e|e^{\prime}}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{R}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{eAsgnAsgn}\par\frac{\mathsf{skip}\mathbin{\mid}c:\mathcal{Q}\land\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}e\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\land V=v\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{Q}\land V\prec v\hskip 8.50012pt\mbox{for all $e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c$ in $gcs$ and all $v\in D$}}{\mathsf{skip}\mathbin{\>\mid\>}\mathsf{do}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od}:\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{Q}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}\mbox{{enab}}(gcs)\raisebox{0.8611pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}}\hskip 8.50012pt\textsc{eSkipDo}

Figure 16.
Some derived rules of ERHL+.
Rules
eLRseq
,
eRLseq
,
eAlgnIf
,
eSeqSkip
,
eIfSkip
,
eDoSkip
, and

eDisjN
are the same as those with corresponding names in
Figure 3
but for
\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}
.

Some derived rules are in
Figure 16
.
Rule
eHavHav
is proposed in
[
AKL+23
]
.
It expresses that
\mathcal{P}
in the initial state must be total as a relation from
x
on the left to
x^{\prime}
on the right.
It can be derived as follows.
Instantiate
eHavSkip
as

\mathsf{hav}\ {x}\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{skip}:(\dot{\forall}x|.\>(\dot{\exists}|x^{\prime}.\>\mathcal{P}))\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\dot{\exists}|x^{\prime}.\>\mathcal{P}
.
Then use
eSeq
with

\mathsf{skip}\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{hav}\ {x}:\dot{\exists}|x.\>P\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}P
(from
eSkipHav
)
to get

\mathsf{hav}\ {x};\mathsf{skip}\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{skip};\mathsf{hav}\ {x^{\prime}}:(\dot{\forall}x|.\>(\dot{\exists}|x^{\prime}.\>\mathcal{P}))\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{P}

Now obtain
eHavHav
by
eRewrite
using skip unit laws.

Aside from
eSkipDo
, the other derived rules in
Figure 16

are like those in
Figure 3
and can be
derived using
eRewrite
. For instance,

eAsgnAsgn
can be derived using

eSkipAsgn
,
eAsgnSkip
,
eSeq
and
eRewrite
.
The
\forall\exists
versions of
rLRseq
and
rRLseq

are both derivable, but
eRLseq
is less useful
than
eLRseq
because the premises of
eRLseq
amount to a strong
\exists\forall
property.
In
subsection 9.3

we show that
eLRseq
is the basis of a Cook complete logic whereas
eRLseq
is not.

Rule
eSkipDo
is derived as follows.
Observe that
\mathsf{skip}\mathrel{\simeq}\mathsf{do}\ {\mathit{false}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathsf{skip}}\ \mathsf{od}
.
So we get the conclusion of
eSkipDo
by
eRewrite
from

\mathsf{do}\ {\mathit{false}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\mathsf{skip}}\ \mathsf{od}\mathbin{\>\mid\>}\mathsf{do}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{od}:\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{Q}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}\mbox{{enab}}(gcs)\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
.
This we prove using
eDo
with
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}:=\mathit{false},\mathit{true}
.
The right-only premises have the form

\mathsf{skip}\mathbin{\mid}c:\mathcal{Q}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\land\mathit{true}\land(V=d)\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{Q}\land(V\prec d)
.
They follow from the premises of
eSkipDo
using
eConseq
to
add the conjunct
\mathit{true}
.
The left-only and joint premises are proved by
eFalse
.
The side condition of this instantiation of
eDo
is

\mathcal{Q}\Rightarrow(\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}\mathit{false}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}=\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}\mbox{{enab}}(gcs)\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$})\lor\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}\mbox{{enab}}(gcs)\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
and the consequent simplifies to true.

{exa}

Recall the example adapted from Unno et al.
[
UTK21
]
, described on page
2
in
section 2
. The judgment
c3\mathbin{\mid}c3:\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}x
specifies possibilistic noninterference. We construct a deductive proof in ERHL+ following heuristics mentioned in
section 2
.

The proof is presented in a goal directed style.
It starts by an application of
eIf
, yielding four obligations, corresponding to the four combinations of the guards
\mathit{high}\neq 0
and
\mathit{high}=0
.

(1)

\mathsf{hav}^{2}\ {x};\mathsf{if}^{3}\ {\ldots}\ \mathsf{fi}\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{hav}^{2}\ {x};\mathsf{if}^{3}\ {\ldots}\ \mathsf{fi}:\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}\mathit{high}\neq 0\mathbin{\mid}\mathit{high}\neq 0\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}x
.

We prove this using a lockstep alignment. Rule
eSeq
is
instantiated with
\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\mathbb{A}x
at the intermediate point. The judgment

\mathsf{hav}^{2}\ {x}\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{hav}^{2}\ {x}:\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}\mathit{high}\neq 0\mathbin{\mid}\mathit{high}\neq 0\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\mathbb{A}x
is obtained
using
eHavHav
and then
eConseq
with the valid implication

\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\Rightarrow(\dot{\forall}x\mbox{\tiny$|$}.\>(\dot{\exists}\mbox{\tiny$|$}x.\>\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\mathbb{A}x))
.
The postcondition
\mathbb{A}x
is chosen to serve as filtering condition.
The judgment

\mathsf{if}^{3}\ {\ldots}\ \mathsf{fi}\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{if}^{3}\ {\ldots}\ \mathsf{fi}:\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\mathbb{A}x\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}x

is proved using the lockstep rules
eAlgnIf

and
eAlgnDo
with loop invariant
\mathbb{A}x
.

(2)

\begin{array}[]{@{}l}x:=^{7}\mathit{low};\mathsf{hav}^{8}\ {b};\mathsf{do}^{9}\ {b\neq 0\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=^{11}x+1\ldots}\ \mathsf{od}\ \mathbin{\mid}\\
x:=^{7}\mathit{low};\mathsf{hav}^{8}\ {b};\mathsf{do}^{9}\ {b\neq 0\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=^{11}x+1\ldots}\ \mathsf{od}\end{array}:\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}\mathit{high}=0\mathbin{\mid}\mathit{high}=0\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}x

This judgment, too, is proved using a lockstep alignment. Key to
the proof is having
\mathbb{A}x\land\mathbb{A}b
as the relational
invariant for the two loops at control point 9. In this derivation

eHavHav
is applied twice with a post-relation that includes
\mathbb{A}b
(again, for filtering).

(3)

\mathsf{hav}^{2}\ {x};\mathsf{if}^{3}\ {\ldots}\ \mathsf{fi}\mathbin{\mid}x:=^{7}\mathit{low};\ldots:\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}\mathit{high}\neq 0\mathbin{\mid}\mathit{high}=0\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}x
.

We prove this using the left-first sequential alignment rule

eLRseq
with intermediate assertion

\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}x\geq\mathit{low}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}
, for which the
premises are:

(a)

\mathsf{hav}^{2}\ {x};\mathsf{if}^{3}\ {\ldots}\ \mathsf{fi}\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{skip}:\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}\mathit{high}\neq 0\mathbin{\mid}\mathit{high}=0\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}x\geq\mathit{low}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}

This judgment is derived using the left-side rules
eSeqSkip
,
eHavSkip
,

eIfSkip
, and
eDoSkip

with invariant
\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}x<\mathit{low}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}
.

(b)

\mathsf{skip}\mathbin{\mid}x:=^{7}\mathit{low};\mathsf{hav}^{8}\ {b};\mathsf{do}^{10}\ {b\neq 0\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}\ldots}\ \mathsf{od}:\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}x\geq\mathit{low}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}x

This is proved using
eSkipSeq
twice, to compose
the following three judgments.
First in the sequence is
\mathsf{skip}\mathbin{\mid}x:=^{7}\mathit{low}:\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}x\geq\mathit{low}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}x\geq\mathit{low}\mathbin{\mid}x=\mathit{low}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }
, proved using
eSkipAssg
and
eConseq
. Second is
\mathsf{skip}\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{hav}^{8}\ {b}:\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}x\geq\mathit{low}\mathbin{\mid}x=\mathit{low}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}b\geq 0\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\land(\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}b\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}=\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}x\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}-\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}x\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$})
, proved by
eSkipHav
and
eConseq
, noting that the precondition implies
(\dot{\exists}\mbox{\tiny$|$}b.\>\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}b\geq 0\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\land(\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}b\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}=\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}x\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}-\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}x\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}))
. Third is
\mathsf{skip}\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{do}^{9}\ {b\neq 0\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=^{10}x+1;\mathsf{hav}^{11}\ {b}}\ \mathsf{od}:\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}b\geq 0\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\land(\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}b\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}=\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}x\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}-\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}x\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$})\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}x
, proved using
eSkipDo
with invariant
\mathcal{Q}:=\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}b\geq 0\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\land(\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}b\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}=\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}x\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}-\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}x\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$})
and variant
V:=abs(\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}b\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$})
. The loop body is proved using
eSkipSeq
,
eSkipAssg
, and
eSkipHav
;
eSkipHav
is instantiated with post-relation
\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}b\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}=\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}x\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}-\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}x\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
.

(4)

x:=^{7}\mathit{low};\mathsf{hav}^{8}\ {b};\mathsf{do}^{9}\ {\ldots}\ \mathsf{od}\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{hav}^{2}\ {x};\mathsf{if}^{3}\ {\ldots}\ \mathsf{fi}:\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}\mathit{high}=0\mathbin{\mid}\mathit{high}\neq 0\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}x
.

We prove this by left-first sequential alignment again, instantiating
eLRseq
with intermediate assertion
\mathbb{A}\mathit{low}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}x\geq\mathit{low}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}
.

Theorem 28
.

All the rules of ERHL+ (
Figure 15
) are sound.

Proof 8.1
.

Soundness proofs of most ERHL+ rules are straightforward. Soundness of

eRewrite
holds for the same reason
Rewrite
and
rRewrite
are
sound: the property (
3
) is about program semantics which is preserved by
\mathrel{\simeq}
.
The soundness proof for
eGhost
relies on the fact that the
ghost

condition ensures erasure of a ghost variable does not influence
termination.

For
eIf
, assume we have the premises:

c\mathbin{\mid}c^{\prime}:\mathcal{R}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}e^{\prime}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{S}

for all
e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c
in
gcs
and
e^{\prime}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c^{\prime}
in
gcs^{\prime}
.
To show the conclusion
\mathsf{if}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{fi}\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{if}\ {gcs^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{fi}:\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{S}
,
consider states
s,s^{\prime},t
such that

(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
and
\llbracket\,\mathsf{if}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{fi}\,\rrbracket\,s\,t
. We must show there
is
t^{\prime}
such that
\llbracket\,\mathsf{if}\ {gcs^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{fi}\,\rrbracket\,s^{\prime}\,t^{\prime}
and

(t,t^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}
.
By semantics there is
e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c
in
gcs
such that

s\in\llbracket\,e\,\rrbracket
and
\llbracket\,c\,\rrbracket\,s\,t
.
By the
total
-
if
condition for
\mathsf{if}\ {gcs^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{fi}
,
there is some
e^{\prime}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c^{\prime}
in
gcs^{\prime}
such that
s^{\prime}\in\llbracket\,e^{\prime}\,\rrbracket
.
By the premise

c\mathbin{\mid}c^{\prime}:\mathcal{R}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}e^{\prime}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\mathcal{S}

there is some
t^{\prime}
with
\llbracket\,c^{\prime}\,\rrbracket\,s^{\prime}\,t^{\prime}
and
(t,t^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}
.

For
eDo
we sketch the argument for loops that have a single guarded command;
a detailed proof is given in
[
NBN23
]
.
The goal is to show

\models\mathsf{do}\ {e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c}\ \mathsf{od}\mathbin{\>\mid\>}\mathsf{do}\ {e^{\prime}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{od}:\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{Q}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}e^{\prime}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}

given the side condition

\mathcal{Q}\Rightarrow(\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}=\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}e^{\prime}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\lor(\mathcal{L}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$})\lor(\mathcal{R}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}e^{\prime}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}))

and premises

\models c\mathbin{\mid}\mathsf{skip}:\mathcal{Q}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\land\mathcal{L}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{Q}
,

\models\mathsf{skip}\mathbin{\mid}c^{\prime}:\mathcal{Q}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}e^{\prime}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\land\mathcal{R}\land(V=v)\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{Q}\land(V\prec v)

(for all
v
), and

\models c\mathbin{\mid}c^{\prime}:\mathcal{Q}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}e^{\prime}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\mathcal{L}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{Q}
.
Consider states
s,s^{\prime},t
such that

(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}
and
\llbracket\,\mathsf{do}\ {e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c}\ \mathsf{od}\,\rrbracket\,s\,t
. We must show there
exists a
t^{\prime}
such that
\llbracket\,\mathsf{do}\ {e^{\prime}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{od}\,\rrbracket\,s^{\prime}\,t^{\prime}
and

(t,t^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}e^{\prime}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
. We proceed by rule induction
on
\llbracket\,\mathsf{do}\ {e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c}\ \mathsf{od}\,\rrbracket\,s\,t
, keeping
s^{\prime}
arbitrary.
In the base case, the run is already terminated:
s\in\mbox{$\lnot$}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}
and
s=t
.
Existence of the required
t^{\prime}
is proved by well-founded induction on
V(s,s^{\prime})
, as follows.
If
s^{\prime}\in\mbox{$\lnot$}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}e^{\prime}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
, we are done by letting
t^{\prime}:=s^{\prime}
.
Otherwise, using
(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}
,
s\in\mbox{$\lnot$}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}
, and the side condition we get the precondition of the right-side premise for
c^{\prime}
. Applying that premise yields some
t^{\prime\prime}
with
(s,t^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}

and
V(s,t^{\prime\prime})\prec V(s,s^{\prime})

and then the inner induction hypothesis yields the required
t^{\prime}
.

In the inductive case, we have
s\in\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}
,
\llbracket\,c\,\rrbracket\,s\,u
, and

\llbracket\,\mathsf{do}\ {e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c}\ \mathsf{od}\,\rrbracket\,u\,t
for some
u
. The inductive hypothesis says
that for any
u^{\prime}
, if
(u,u^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}
, then there is a
t^{\prime}
such that

\llbracket\,\mathsf{do}\ {e^{\prime}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{od}\,\rrbracket u^{\prime}t^{\prime}
and

(t,t^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}e^{\prime}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
.
If
(s,s^{\prime})
satisfy the precondition for the left-only or joint premise then applying the premise
yields
t^{\prime}
(reached from
s^{\prime}
by zero or one iterations of
c^{\prime}
) such that
(u,t^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}
whence the inductive hypothesis yields our goal. Otherwise, by
(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}
and the side condition,
(s,s^{\prime})
satisfies the precondition for the right-only premise. But that does not immediately enable use of the inductive hypothesis. So we show by well founded induction on
V(s,s^{\prime})
that there exists some
t^{\prime\prime}
,
reached by some number of iterations of
c^{\prime}
, with
(s,t^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}

and

(s,t^{\prime\prime})
satisfies either the left-only or joint premise.
Either of those premises, together with the main induction hypothesis and reachability of
t^{\prime\prime}
,
yields the goal.

8.1.
Digression on control determinacy

Our GCL has two forms of nondeterminacy. The language has havoc, which makes an unboundedly nondeterministic choice of a value. This can serve to model randomization as well as input data.
It also has nondeterminacy in terms of control.
This does not necessarily lead to nondeterministic outcomes.
For example, the command

\mathsf{if}^{1}\ {true\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=^{2}y\talloblong true\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=^{3}y}\ \mathsf{fi}

satisfies

\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}y\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}=\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}y\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>$}}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}x\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}=\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}x\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}

but its control flows nondeterministically to either the point labelled 2 or the point labelled 3.
On the other hand,
d3^{\prime}
below has nondeterministic outcomes.

d3\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\mathsf{if}\ {true\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=0}\ \mathsf{fi}\quad\qquad d3^{\prime}\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\mathsf{if}\ {true\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=0\talloblong true\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=1}\ \mathsf{fi}

A command
c
is called
control deterministic
provided that
the guards are mutually exclusive,
for every if- and do-command in
c
.
A control deterministic
\mathsf{if}\ {e_{0}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c_{0}\talloblong e_{1}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c_{1}\talloblong\ldots}\ \mathsf{fi}

is equivalent to the nested if-else
\mathsf{if}\ {e_{0}}\ \mathsf{then}\ {c_{0}}\ \mathsf{else}\ {\mathsf{if}\ {e_{1}}\ \mathsf{then}\ {c_{1}}\ \mathsf{else}\ {\ldots}\ \mathsf{fi}}\ \mathsf{fi}
.
A control deterministic do-command can be represented similarly.

For a command
\mathsf{if}\ {e_{0}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c_{0}\talloblong e_{1}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c_{1}\ldots}\ \mathsf{fi}
that is not control deterministic, one can make it so in the form

\mathsf{if}\ {e_{0}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c_{0}\talloblong e_{1}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}e_{0}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c_{1}\ldots}\ \mathsf{fi}

but this may eliminate some behaviors, as is the case for
d3^{\prime}
above.
To retain all behaviors one can use havoc with an extra variable
that serves to prophesize the choice, as in

d3^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\mathsf{hav}\ {z};\mathsf{if}\ {true\land z=0\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=0\talloblong true\land z\neq 0\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=1}\ \mathsf{fi}

where
z
is fresh.
Conventional if/else and while commands are control deterministic,
and the normal form construction of
subsection 5.2
preserves control determinacy.
So there is little reason to dwell on programs that are not control deterministic. Nonetheless we briefly consider the following limitation of ERHL+ for such programs.

This
\forall\exists
judgment is valid:

d3\mathbin{\mid}d3^{\prime}:true\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}x
.
But rule
eIf
is not directly applicable,
because one of the premises would be
x:=0\mathbin{\mid}x:=1:true\land true\land true\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}x

which is false.
One might guess to change the rule simply by existentially quantifying the right side guarded commands but this is unsound.
For example, consider

d4\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\mathsf{if}\ {true\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=0}\ \mathsf{fi}\quad\qquad d4^{\prime}\mathrel{\,\hat{=}\,}\mathsf{if}\ {x\geq 0\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=0\talloblong x\leq 0\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}x:=1}\ \mathsf{fi}.

For every guarded command on the left, there is one on the right that relates according to the
spec
true\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}x
where
e
is the guard.
In particular:

x:=0\mathbin{\mid}x:=0:true\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}x\geq 0\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}x
.
But it is not the case that
d4\mathbin{\mid}d4^{\prime}
satisfies

true\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}x
.
What is needed is to existentially quantify a subset of guarded commands on the right that covers all cases.

\frac{\parbox{293.2521pt}{For all $e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c$ in $gcs$ there is some $g\subseteq gcs^{\prime}$ such that
$\mathcal{R}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\Rightarrow\mbox{{enab}}(g)$ and \quad
\hskip 40.00006pt $c\mathbin{\mid}c^{\prime}:\mathcal{R}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}e^{\prime}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{S}$
for all $e^{\prime}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c^{\prime}$ in $g$
}}{\mathsf{if}\ {gcs}\ \mathsf{fi}\mathbin{\>\mid\>}\mathsf{if}\ {gcs^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{fi}:\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{S}}\quad\textsc{right=eIfX}

The side condition
\mathcal{R}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\Rightarrow\mbox{{enab}}(g)
ensures the set
g
covers all cases.
Note that there is no such set that can be used
to show the invalid judgment
d4\mathbin{\mid}d4^{\prime}:true\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}x
.
Rule
eIfX
does yield
d3\mathbin{\mid}d3^{\prime}:true\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathbb{A}x
.
A similar issue arises for the joint premises in rule
eDo
,
and one can address it in the same way as
eIfX
.
The rule
eDoX
replaces the joint premise of
eDo
with the following:

\begin{array}[t]{l}\mbox{For all $e\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c$ in $gcs$ there is some $g\subseteq gcs^{\prime}$ such that
$\mathcal{Q}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\mathcal{L}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\mathcal{R}\Rightarrow\mbox{{enab}}(g)$}\\
\mbox{\quad and for all $e^{\prime}\mathrel{\shortrightarrow}c^{\prime}$ in $g$ we have
$c\mathbin{\mid}c^{\prime}:\mathcal{Q}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}\land\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}e^{\prime}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\mathcal{L}\land\mbox{$\lnot$}\mathcal{R}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{Q}$}\end{array}

We choose the simpler rules
rIf
and
rDo
to streamline the presentation, at the cost of restricting to control deterministic programs when necessary,
specifically
34
(a).
We conjecture the restriction can be dropped, using rules
eIfX
and
eDoX
.
There is little practical motivation because conventional control structures (if/else and while) are control deterministic.

9.
Filtered alignment automata and alignment completeness of ERHL+

This section introduces a form of alignment automaton suited to
\forall\exists
properties. The logic ERHL+ is shown, in

subsection 9.2
, to be alignment complete with respect to these automata.

9.1.
Filtered alignment automata

For
\forall\exists
reasoning, the form of alignment given by
subsection 4.1
is unsatisfactory: if the right program is nondeterministic, there needs to be a way to keep some but not all its transitions for right-only and joint steps of the product.
An example is
c3
in
section 2
, which we explore further in the sequel.
First we adapt
subsection 4.1
of alignment
product to include an additional state relation which serves to filter product executions.

{defi}

Suppose
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J)
is an alignment automata as in

subsection 4.1
. Let
keep set

K
be a set of states,
i.e.,
K\subseteq(Ctrl\times Ctrl^{\prime})\times(Sto\times Sto^{\prime})
. The
filtered alignment
automaton

\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J,K)
is

((Ctrl\times Ctrl^{\prime}),(Sto\times Sto^{\prime}),(\mathit{init},\mathit{init}^{\prime}),(\mathit{fin},\mathit{fin}^{\prime}),\Mapsto_{K})

where
\Mapsto_{K}
is defined by:

\sigma\Mapsto_{K}\tau
iff
\tau\in K
and

\sigma\Mapsto\tau
(with
\sigma,\tau
ranging over states).

Here the un-subscripted
\Mapsto
refers to the relation in
subsection 4.1
.
Note that
K
is used in
\Mapsto_{K}
to filter states that the product steps
to. As a consequence,
[\mathit{init}\mid\mathit{init}^{\prime}]\lor K
is always a true-invariant of
\Mapsto_{K}
.
Apart from this use of
K
, the transition relation is the same as
the relation
\Mapsto
for unfiltered alignment automata (
subsection 4.1
).

{exa}

Fig.
17
shows a filtered product for
c3
, the running example on
\forall\exists
properties adapted from
[
UTK21
]
—
see page
2
in
section 2
, and
Figure 6
.
Fig.
17
depicts possible transitions of the product, given
L,R,J
conditions that capture the following alignment: lockstep if the two executions agree on the test
\mathit{high}\neq 0
(abbreviated as
h
in Fig.
17
); left-first sequential otherwise. This is similar to the deductive proof in
section 8
.
Automata edges are labeled with tests or commands. We write
\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}c\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }
,
\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}c\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}
, and
\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}c\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
to mean that
c
takes place on both sides, on the left, and on the right, respectively. The label
f
abbreviates the final control point 12. Filter
K
is
\mathit{true}
everywhere except at control points marked in the figure with green dashed boxes. For example, the step from
(2,2)
to
(3,3)
which havocs
x
on both sides is filtered by
\mathbb{A}x
. The step to control point
(f,9)
is filtered so that the value of
\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}b\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
is the same as the difference between the values of
x
on both sides.

The figure does not depict transitions that cannot take place. In particular, vertices for control points
(f,5)
and
(f,6)
are missing. These vertices correspond to the diverging loop in the example taking place on the right. The filter
\mathbb{A}x
on
(f,3)
, in conjunction with invariant

[f|3]\Rightarrow\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.0pt[$}x\geq\mathit{low}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt]$}

ensures that the transition from
(f,4)
to
(f,5)
which would be guarded by
\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$[\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$}x<\mathit{low}\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.0pt\rangle$}
cannot occur.
∎

Filtered product for example
c3
.
Non-trivial filter conditions are shown in green dashed boxes; the filter is
\mathit{true}
at all other control points.
Notation
\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }
abbreviates
\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$\langle\hskip-2.1pt[$}e\mathbin{\mid}e\raisebox{1.07639pt}{$]\hskip-2.1pt\rangle$ }
,
and we use similar notation for commands.

Adequacy of filtered alignment automata

{defi}

Let
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J,K)
be a filtered alignment automata and let

\mathcal{Q}\subseteq(Sto\times Sto^{\prime})
. The filtered automata is
\mathcal{Q}
-adequate in
the
\forall\exists
sense
provided for all
(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}
and
t
with

(\mathit{init},s)\mapsto^{*}(\mathit{fin},t)
, there exists a
t^{\prime}
such that

((\mathit{init},\mathit{init}^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\Mapsto^{*}_{K}((\mathit{fin},\mathit{fin}^{\prime}),(t,t^{\prime})).

Lemma 29
.

Given automata
A
,
A^{\prime}
and store relations
\mathcal{Q}
,
\mathcal{S}
,
if
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J,K)
is
\mathcal{Q}
-adequate in the
\forall\exists
sense and

\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J,K)\models\mathcal{Q}\leadsto\mathcal{S}
, then

A,A^{\prime}\models\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{S}
.

Proof 9.1
.

To prove
A,A^{\prime}\models\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{S}
, consider any
s,s^{\prime},t
such that

(\mathit{init},s)\mapsto^{*}(\mathit{fin},t)
and
(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}
.
By
\mathcal{Q}
-adequacy there is a state
t^{\prime}
such that

((\mathit{init},\mathit{init}^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\Mapsto_{K}^{*}((\mathit{fin},\mathit{fin}^{\prime}),(t,t^{\prime}))
. By taking the right
projection of this trace, and destuttering, we obtain a trace

(\mathit{init}^{\prime},s^{\prime})\mapsto^{\prime*}(\mathit{fin}^{\prime},t^{\prime})
of
A^{\prime}
.
(Stuttering steps can arise from left-only steps of the product.)
Now from
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J,K)\models\mathcal{Q}\leadsto\mathcal{S}
we
have
(t,t^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}
.

The lemma suggests an approach for verifying
\forall\exists

properties: to show
A,A^{\prime}\models\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{S}
,
construct a filtered product, prove it is adequate and
satisfies the partial correctness spec

\mathcal{Q}\leadsto\mathcal{S}
.
The latter amounts to proving a
\forall\forall
property of the set of trace pairs represented by the product.
As in the
\forall\forall
setting, a proof method needs to connect adequacy with annotations and alignment conditions,
in a way that can be checked modularly.
To this end, we state the key definition and then explain its elements.

{defi}

Given
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J,K)
with annotation
an
,
we say the automaton has
adequate filtering for
an
iff there exists a function

V:(Ctrl\times Ctrl^{\prime})\times(Sto\times Sto^{\prime})\to D
where
(D,\prec)
is
a well-ordered set, such that the following four conditions hold.
(Where
\mapsto,\mapsto^{\prime}
are the transition relations of
A,A^{\prime}
.)

Left-permissive
:

For any
n,n^{\prime},s,s^{\prime},m,t
,
if
(s,s^{\prime})\in an(n,n^{\prime})
and
((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in L
and
(n,s)\mapsto(m,t)
then
((m,n^{\prime}),(t,s^{\prime}))\in K
.

Joint-productive
:

For any
n,n^{\prime},s,s^{\prime},m,t
, if
(s,s^{\prime})\in an(n,n^{\prime})
and

((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in J
and
(n,s)\!\mapsto\!(m,t)
then there are
m^{\prime},t^{\prime}
such that

(n^{\prime},s^{\prime})\!\mapsto^{\prime}\!(m^{\prime},t^{\prime})
and
((m,m^{\prime}),(t,t^{\prime}))\in K
.

Right-productive
:

For any
n,n^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}
, if

(s,s^{\prime})\in an(n,n^{\prime})
and
((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))\in R
then there are
m^{\prime},t^{\prime}
such that
(n^{\prime},s^{\prime})\!\mapsto^{\prime}\!(m^{\prime},t^{\prime})

and
((n,m^{\prime}),(s,t^{\prime}))\in K
and
V((n,m^{\prime}),(s,t^{\prime}))\prec V((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))
.

Enabled
:

For any
n,n^{\prime}
, the implication
\breve{an}(n,n^{\prime})\Rightarrow L\lor R\lor J\lor[\mathit{fin}|\mathit{fin}^{\prime}]
is valid.

We sometimes say “
K
is an
adequate filtering for
an
” to emphasize the key role of
K
.
We briefly explain why these conditions ensure adequacy in the
\forall\exists
sense, if the annotation
an
is valid.
First, the
enabled
condition ensures the underlying
L,R,J
-product
can always perform left-only, right-only, or joint steps; this is the same as
in
section 7
for
\forall\forall
.
But transitions by
\Mapsto_{K}
are filtered by the keep set
K
,
so we need to ensure that it keeps enough.
Transitions of
A
can only be covered by the alignment product using LO or JO steps.
The
left-permissive
condition applies to states
((n,n^{\prime}),(s,s^{\prime}))

where the product is poised to perform an LO step, and requires that it be allowed by
K
.
The
joint-productive
condition applies if the product is ready to take a JO step. It requires that for any
A
transition, there is some
A^{\prime}
transition such that the joint step is allowed by
K
.
Finally, the
right-productive
condition ensures that
for RO steps,
K
is not just keeping divergent traces of
A^{\prime}
.
When the product is poised to take a RO step,
K
must allow some transition
that decreases the value of the variant
V
.

The following results parallel
1
,
4
, and

5
.

Lemma 30
.

Let
an
be a valid annotation of

\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J,K)
for
\mathcal{P}\leadsto\mathcal{Q}

and suppose
K
is an adequate filtering for
an
.
Then
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J,K)
is
\mathcal{P}
-adequate in the
\forall\exists
sense.

Proof 9.2
.

First we make a simple observation that pertains to any automaton
A

and annotation
an
for some spec
P\leadsto Q
.
If
an
is valid,
s
is in
P
, and
(\mathit{init},s)\mapsto^{*}(m,t)
,
then
t\in an(m)
.
Thus for the product
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J,K)
, the condition
an(n,n^{\prime})
holds whenever the product is at control point
(n,n^{\prime})
.
Hence
L\lor R\lor J\lor[\mathit{fin}|\mathit{fin}^{\prime}]
is an invariant of the product,
owing to the enabled condition in
subsection 9.1
of adequate filtering.

To show that
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J,K)
is
\mathcal{P}
-adequate in the
\forall\exists
sense,
suppose
(s,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}
and suppose
(\mathit{init},s)\mapsto^{*}(\mathit{fin},t)
, i.e., there is a terminated trace

\tau
of
A
from
s
to
t
.
We must show that the product can simulate
\tau
, without getting stuck, until it reaches
(\mathit{fin},\mathit{fin}^{\prime})
.
Because
L\lor R\lor J\lor[\mathit{fin}|\mathit{fin}^{\prime}]
is invariant,
and using the liveness of
L
,
R
, and
J
as required by
subsection 4.1

(via
subsection 9.1
),
the product can keep taking steps until it reaches
(\mathit{fin},\mathit{fin}^{\prime})
.
The LO and JO steps move forward simulating
\tau
, thus eventually matching all of
\tau
,
unless the product diverges taking only RO steps.
Right-productivity ensures that only finitely many RO steps can happen before
L\lor J\lor[\mathit{fin}|\mathit{fin}^{\prime}]
holds.

Making this precise requires a slightly intricate induction hypothesis. A detailed proof can be found in
[
NBN23
]
.

Theorem 31

(semantic soundness and completeness of filtered automata)
.

We have

A,A^{\prime}\models\mathcal{P}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{Q}
iff there are
L,R,J,K
and a
valid annotation
an
of
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J,K)
for
\mathcal{P}\leadsto\mathcal{Q}

such that
K
is an adequate filtering for
an
.
Moreover, if
A,A^{\prime}
act on variable stores,
and
A,A^{\prime},\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}
are finitely supported,
then there are finitely supported such
L,R,J,K,an
(and witness
V
).

Proof 9.3
.

(Sketch)
The proof is by mutual implication.
For right-implies-left the argument is easy.
If
an
is a valid annotation of
\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J,K)
for
\mathcal{P}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{Q}

and
K
is an adequate filtering for
an
,
then by Lemma
30
the product is
\mathcal{P}
-adequate.
Since
an
is valid for
\mathcal{P}\leadsto\mathcal{Q}
we have

\prod(A,A^{\prime},L,R,J,K)\models\mathcal{P}\leadsto\mathcal{Q}
by Lemma
3
.
So by Lemma
29
we have
A,A^{\prime}\models\mathcal{P}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{Q}
.

For left-implies-right,
suppose
A,A^{\prime}\models\mathcal{P}\mathrel{\mbox{\footnotesize$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\exists}}{{\raisebox{-0.24113pt}{$\thickapprox$}\hskip-4.13332pt>}}$}}\mathcal{Q}
.
The idea is to use a left-first sequential alignment automaton such that,
once the left-only execution has finished, with final store
t
,
the filter condition only keeps states that are in some terminating run of

A^{\prime}
that ends in a store
t^{\prime}
such that
(t,t^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}
.
The variant is defined in terms of shortest terminating runs that end in
\mathcal{Q}
.
The details are somewhat complicated and relegated to an appendix
for the interested reader (
Appendix D
).
